=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a sequence A = (A_1,\ldots,A_N). You can perform the following two types of operations any number of times in any order:

- Let K be the length of A just before the operation. Choose an integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq K-1, and swap the i-th and (i+1)-th elements of A.
- Let K be the length of A just before the operation. Choose an integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq K and all the values from the 1-st through the i-th elements of A are equal, and delete all the elements from the 1-st through the i-th of A.

Find the minimum total number of operations required to make A an empty sequence.
You are given T test cases; solve each of them.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Each case is given in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answer for each test case in order, separated by newlines.

Constraints


- 1\leq T\leq 10^5
- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq N
- The sum of N over all test cases is at most 2\times 10^5.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
5
1 1 2 1 2
4
4 2 1 3
11
1 2 1 2 1 2 1 2 1 2 1

Sample Output 1

3
4
8

For the 1st test case, A can be made empty by the following three operations:

- Swap the 3rd and 4th elements of A. Now, A is (1,1,1,2,2).
- Delete the 1st through 3rd elements of A. Now, A is (2,2).
- Delete the 1st through 2nd elements of A. Now, A is an empty sequence.

For the 2nd test case, A can be made empty by deleting the 1st element four times. Also, it is impossible to make A empty in three or fewer operations.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

We can model binary alternating as choosing labels. Since values alternate, labels for 1s odd, 2s even if start 1. We can choose labels to minimize.

This is equivalent to partitioning positions into blocks of same parity with increasing block order. Since labels are ordered, and same parity blocks can be multiple. The cost contributed by block B with later blocks is number of elements of later blocks that appear before elements of B? Let's derive from first principles.

We have labels. Let's define for each value v, the occurrences in original order. If we assign all occurrences of v to blocks, the block indices for v form a nondecreasing? Actually if an occurrence of v has label a and a later occurrence has label b. If a > b, then pair (earlier, later) same value has inversion. Is that ever beneficial? It means earlier occurrence deleted later than later occurrence. Since same value, could we swap their labels (assign earlier b, later a) to reduce inversions? Let's check. For two occurrences of same value at positions p<q with labels a>b (inversion). If we swap labels: earlier gets b, later gets a. Since both same value, block membership changes but each block remains monochromatic. How does inversions with other elements change? This is like swapping two labels in the label sequence at positions p,q. The number of inversions changes by something. Maybe we can always assume labels for same value are nondecreasing with positions (earlier occurrence gets <= later label). Because if a>b, swapping them might not increase cost? Let's analyze.

Given label sequence L, positions p<q with same value and L_p > L_q. Swap labels at p,q to L'_p = L_q, L'_q = L_p. Since values same, validity preserved. Distinct labels count unchanged. Inversions change. For any other position r:
- If r between p and q: Consider pairs (p,r) and (r,q) originally; after swap, labels at p and q swapped. This is equivalent to swapping two elements in the label sequence. The inversion count of a sequence changes by an odd? Actually swapping two adjacent elements changes inv by ±1; swapping non-adjacent can change by something. But because L_p > L_q, does swapping to put smaller before larger reduce inversions? For any sequence, swapping an inverted pair (larger before smaller) to sorted order (smaller before larger) decreases inversion count by 1 + 2*? Let's compute. Let labels a > b at positions p<q. Swap to b,a. For any r between p,q with label c:
Pairs involving p/q and r:
Original contributions:
(p,r): inversion if a > c.
(r,q): inversion if c > b.
New:
(p,r): b > c?
(r,q): c > a?
Difference = [b>c + c>a] - [a>c + c>b].
Since a>b. Let's enumerate c relative:
c < b: original a>c yes, c>b no =>1; new b>c yes, c>a no =>1 diff0.
c > a: original a>c no, c>b yes =>1; new b>c no, c>a yes =>1 diff0.
b < c < a: original a>c yes, c>b yes =>2; new b>c no, c>a no =>0 diff -2.

Maybe there is a dynamic programming with "minimum cost to delete prefix" using a priority queue? Let's revisit the queue process. At each step, we choose a value and pop a prefix of its remaining occurrences. The cost to pop c occurrences of value x is the number of remaining non-x elements before these c x's. This cost is exactly the number of elements that will be "skipped" by this block. If we think of the final deletion sequence D, each block of x in D corresponds to popping c x's. The number of inversions contributed by this block is the number of elements that appear before these x's in original but after the block in D. In the queue process, when we pop the block, we pay the number of remaining elements before the block's elements; these are exactly elements that will be after the block in D (later blocks). So cost is paid at pop time. This is like: We have queues for each value. We repeatedly select a queue and pop a prefix. The cost of popping a prefix is the number of elements in other queues that are currently ahead of the popped elements. After popping, those other elements remain and will be popped later. This is exactly the cost of "merging" queues where the cost of an element crossing is counted when the earlier-in-D element is popped from behind.

This process is analogous to computing the minimum cost to merge sorted lists where each list is the occurrence positions of a value, and we can pop multiple from one list at a time. The cost of popping c from list x is the number of elements from other lists that lie before the c-th popped element? Wait sum over each popped element of other elements before it. This is equal to the total number of pairs (popped element, other element) where other element is before popped element. In terms of positions, if we pop c elements from list x, and the positions of these c elements in the current merged remaining sequence are p1<...<pc, cost = sum(p_r - r). This can be computed as (sum p_r) - c(c+1)/2. The p_r are the positions of the next c occurrences of x in the remaining sequence. This cost is the number of elements before the last popped? No.

If we pop one element at a time, cost for popping the next x is the number of remaining elements before it. If we pop c together, cost is larger than popping just the first? It is sum of costs for each popped element before any deletion? Actually if we popped them one by one and deleted each immediately, after deleting the first x, the second x's cost would be number of remaining elements before it, which is p2 - 1 (since first x removed) instead of p2 - 2? Let's compare:
- Pop c together: cost = sum_{r=1}^c (p_r - r) (because before popping, p_r includes r-1 selected x's before it; non-x before = p_r - r).
- Pop one by one: after deleting first x, positions shift; cost for second = (p2-1) - 1? Let's compute: original p2, after removing first x, its position is p2-1, non-x before = (p2-1) - 1 = p2-2. So cost is same whether popped together or one by one? But if popped together, we bring all c to front before deleting; the swaps cost is sum(p_r - r). If popped one by one, after deleting each, the next x is already closer; total swaps same. But deletion cost differs: together 1 deletion, one-by-one c deletions. The swap cost to bring them to front one by one is independent of whether we delete them together. Thus the only benefit of grouping is saving c-1 deletion operations, with no extra swap cost! Suppose current remaining sequence: x y x z. Pop two x's together. Positions p1=1,p2=3. Cost = (1-1)+(3-2)=1 (y before second x). Operations: swap y and second x -> x x y z, delete x's. D order of original elements: first x, second x, y, z. So D has x,x adjacent, which would be one block if we didn't delete separately. We could have deleted them together after same swaps. So grouping consecutive in D is free. This suggests we can always merge any two blocks of the same value that are adjacent in D without increasing swaps. Therefore optimal D has no adjacent same-color blocks (as we said). But the queue process "pop c together" vs "pop one by one" with same D order (x,x,y,z) would merge them. The swap cost is same. So grouping saves deletions.

This insight: The swap cost depends only on the order in which individual elements are deleted (D), not on how they are grouped into deletion operations. Deletion cost is number of runs in D. Therefore the problem is: Choose a permutation D of elements that respects per-color original order, minimizing inv(A,D) + runs(D). We already knew.

Now, since grouping adjacent same-color in D is free, we can think of D as a sequence of elements; runs are maximal equal adjacent. We want minimize inversions + runs. This is exactly the label model.

Can we choose D to minimize inversions + runs? This is like we want D to be close to A (small inversions) and have few runs. Since A itself has runs r(A) and inv0, cost r(A). We can reorder to reduce runs at cost inversions. This is a "minimum cost to reduce runs" problem.

This suggests a much simpler operational strategy: We can perform adjacent swaps; each swap can reduce the number of runs by at most 2. Let's formalize run reduction. Let R be number of runs. Initially R0 = runs(A). After any sequence of s adjacent swaps, final runs R_f. Total operations = s + R_f (delete each final run). Thus we need a more general run-reduction perspective. Each swap has Δ ∈ {-2,-1,0,...}. Cost change = 1+Δ. A Δ=-2 swap saves1, Δ=-1 neutral, Δ>=0 harmful. - Δ=-2: saving1
- Δ=-1: saving0
- Δ=0: cost1
- Δ>0: cost >1.
So only Δ=-2 swaps produce positive saving; Δ=-1 are free (no net cost) but can enable Δ=-2. We need maximize number of Δ=-2 swaps, with Δ=-1 swaps allowed for free, and avoid harmful swaps. Alternative approach: The run-reduction process is equivalent to sorting by adjacent swaps; the final runs R_f. The total cost = s + R_f. Since R_f >= 1, cost >= s+1. But savings from initial runs. We can think of each swap as an operation that may reduce the number of runs. The maximum possible reduction in runs for a given number of swaps is at most 2s, so cost >= R0 - s. Maybe there is a direct formula involving "maximum number of disjoint alternating quadruples" that can be eliminated? Let's analyze run reduction in terms of runs.

A sequence can be represented by its run-length encoding: values v_1, v_2, ..., v_R with adjacent values different. Adjacent swaps of elements within runs? Swapping equal elements no effect. To reduce runs, we swap elements at boundaries. A swap of adjacent elements from different runs (i.e., at a boundary between run j and j+1) can change the run sequence. Let's represent only the sequence of run values (length R). Since swapping adjacent elements at a boundary between two runs of values x and y may:
- If the run of x has length >1 and run of y has length >1? Swapping one element from each run? Actually adjacent elements at boundary are last x and first y. Swapping them changes the run values sequence. The effect on R depends on neighboring run values. In terms of run values, a swap at boundary between x and y can be seen as swapping the boundary elements; if runs have lengths, it may merge/split. The maximum Δ=-2 occurs when the run value sequence has pattern y, x, y, x? Wait earlier pattern y x y x at element level corresponds to run values? The pattern at element level required y x y x with all four elements? In run terms, the runs involved must have length1? Let's analyze.

A swap of adjacent elements at a boundary between runs x and y. Let the run of x have length Lx, run of y length Ly. The swapped elements are last x and first y. After swap, the last x becomes y and first y becomes x. This can merge with neighboring runs if the new y at end of x-run matches the next run after y? Wait the x-run is before y-run. Let the run before x-run have value a (if any), and run after y-run have value b (if any). Run sequence around: a, x (len Lx), y (len Ly), b. After swapping boundary elements:

So the process on run-length encoding: We have runs with values and lengths. A beneficial swap is between two adjacent runs that both have length 1, and their outer neighbors match the opposite values (pattern y,x,y,x). A free operation (Δ=-1) occurs when only one outer neighbor matches. Pattern: A=y, B=x, C=y, D=z (z != x). Swap B,C -> y (a+1), x, z (d). So type1 can be seen as deleting C and then merging B with D (which also removes B from run sequence), saving1. More generally:
- Free deletion: If there is a singleton run C such that the run two positions to its left (A) has the same value, and the run immediately to its right (D) has a different value from the run immediately to its left (B), then delete C. Cost unchanged.
- If D has the same value as B, then deleting C causes B and D to merge, so both C and B disappear from run sequence; this is a type1 saving1. But wait B is not deleted, it merges with D; the run count decreases by2 (C removed, B and D merge). This is exactly type1.

Similarly, we could delete a singleton run C that has an equal run two positions to its right, with analogous conditions.

Thus the problem reduces to: We have a sequence of runs with lengths. We can delete singleton runs for free if they have an equal run at distance 2 and the intervening run's neighbor on the other side is different (so no merge). If the neighbor on the other side is equal, we get a "double deletion" that saves1. This is like a string reduction where singleton runs can be removed, and when removal would cause two equal runs to become adjacent, they merge (reducing run count by an extra 1 and saving1).

Thus the core operation: Choose a singleton run C such that there is a run A of the same value two positions to the left, and the run B between them is singleton. Then we can "remove" C (and if the run D immediately after C has the same value as B, also remove D and gain +1 saving). This is like a stack reduction: when we see a run equal to the run two positions back, and the run between is singleton, we can pop the current run (and maybe next).

That would mean runs1-4 type1, invalid. So why did stack algorithm still give correct score? Maybe because it effectively shifted the window: after reducing [1,2,1,2] to [1,2], the stack [1,2] represents runs2,3 after the actual second swap, not runs1,2. The algorithm doesn't care about absolute positions; it just maintains a reduced sequence of run values where the last two runs are the "active frontier". The score count matched, but the mapping is not direct. It might be a valid abstract reduction system even if the intermediate stack values don't correspond to a contiguous prefix of the actual run sequence? Need ensure the stack algorithm's operations are realizable in the original sequence.

Let's test stack algorithm on a case where it might overcount. We need verify with brute force for small sequences mentally or by reasoning.

Stack algorithm rules:
Scan run values V. Maintain stack S.
For each new value x:
- Append x to S.
- While the last 4 elements of S form pattern a,b,a,b (i.e., S[-4]=S[-2], S[-3]=S[-1]), replace them by a,b (i.e., pop last two) and increment score.
- Else if the last 3 elements of S form pattern a,b,a (S[-3]=S[-1]), then pop the last element (free deletion) (i.e., replace a,b,a by a,b). But careful: if we pop last, then the new x is gone; we don't append? Actually the free deletion deletes the newly appended run if it equals two positions back. So after appending x, if S[-3]=S[-1] and not type1, pop S[-1]. This reduces length by1.
- Continue checking.

This is similar to maintaining a stack with no pattern a,b,a at the end? Let's simulate alternating:
V=1,2,1,2,1,2,1,2,1,2,1.
S=[]
1 -> [1]
2 -> [1,2]
1 -> [1,2,1]
2 -> append [1,2,1,2]; type1 -> pop2 -> [1,2], score1.
1 -> [1,2,1]
2 -> [1,2,1,2]; type1 -> [1,2], score2.
1 -> [1,2,1]
2 -> [1,2,1,2]; type1 -> [1,2], score3.
1 -> [1,2,1]. End. Score3. Good.

Example 1 2 1 3 2 3:
1->[1]
2->[1,2]
1->[1,2,1]
3->append [1,2,1,3]; no type1; check last3 [2,1,3] no; last3 [1,2,1]? Actually after appending, S=[1,2,1,3]. The newly appended 3 does not equal S[-3]=2? S[-3]=2, S[-1]=3 no. But there is a free deletion of the previous run (the second 1) before appending 3? In the actual process, we deleted run3 (1) when we saw D=3, because pattern 1,2,1,3. The stack algorithm should detect this when appending 3: before appending 3, S=[1,2,1]. The new x=3; since S[-3]=S[-1]=1 and S[-2]=2 != x=3, we should pop S[-1] (the second 1) and then append x? Wait free deletion of C (the second 1) occurs when D (new x) is not equal to B (2). The resulting sequence is A,B,D = 1,2,3. So the stack after processing 3 should be [1,2,3], not [1,2,1,3]. So the rule: when appending x, if S[-3]=S[-1] (pattern a,b,a) and S[-2] != x, then pop S[-1] (delete the second a) and then append x? But if we pop first, S becomes [1,2], then append 3 -> [1,2,3]. Yes. If S[-2] == x (pattern a,b,a,b), then type1: pop last two? Actually S=[1,2,1], x=2; type1 replaces [1,2,1,2] with [1,2], so pop S[-1] and don't append x? Wait append x gives [1,2,1,2]; pop last two -> [1,2]. Equivalent to: before appending, if S[-3]=S[-1] and S[-2]=x, then pop S[-1] (remove second a) and do not append x? Let's see S=[1,2,1], x=2. Pop S[-1] -> [1,2]; x not appended, result [1,2]. Yes. If S[-2] != x, pop S[-1] then append x -> [1,2,3]. So the rule can be applied before appending: when the top of stack has pattern a,b,a and the next input is x:
- If x == b: replace a,b,a by a,b (score+1), consume x.
- If x != b: replace a,b,a by a,b,x (free deletion), consume x.
This is exactly a stack reduction where we process input and whenever the stack top three form a,b,a, we reduce based on next input.

But what if after popping and appending, new patterns appear at the top? We should loop.

Let's test with example 1 2 1 3 2 3 using this stack algorithm:

read2: S=[1,2]
read1: S=[1,2,1]

Alternating:
S after reading 1,2,1; next x=2, top3 1,2,1, x==b -> pop -> [1,2], score1. Continue.

This stack algorithm is promising. It processes the run value sequence and counts type1 reductions. It ignores run lengths, but maybe it correctly models the optimal because the stack represents the reduced run sequence where the top runs are always active? Wait in the stack [1,2] after a type1, the runs have length2, but the algorithm treats them as if they could be part of a future a,b,a pattern. In alternating, after first reduction S=[1,2], then read1 -> [1,2,1]. This top3 pattern 1,2,1 corresponds to actual runs? We need prove the stack algorithm gives the maximum score. This is likely a known greedy for this rewriting system. Let's test on a potential counterexample where stack algorithm overcounts.

Stack algorithm overcounted because it allowed free deletion with B inactive.

So we need a stack algorithm that tracks active status (singleton) of runs. The stack values alone insufficient.

Let's refine. We need process runs with lengths. Initially all runs length1 (active). When a run absorbs another, its length increases and it becomes inactive. In the stack, we can store for each run whether it is active (len=1) or inactive (len>1). Operations require the middle run B to be active, and the run C to be active. A (left outer) can be any; D can be any. After type1, A and B become inactive (if not already), C and D are removed. After free deletion, A becomes inactive, C removed, B remains active.

This is like a stack of items with values and active flags. We scan runs left to right, maintaining a reduced sequence. When we append a new run (which is active), we can apply reductions at the end of the stack if the last few items match patterns with active middle. Let's design stack reduction with active flags.

We process original runs one by one. Each original run is active (length1). When we append it to stack, it may trigger reductions involving the end of the stack. The stack represents the current reduced run sequence (after some operations on the prefix processed so far). We want maximize type1 count. We can use free deletions greedily? Need ensure optimal.

Let's define an item as (value, active). Initially active=True. A run becomes inactive when it absorbs another run (length>1). Inactive runs cannot be B or C in reductions, but can be A or D.

Reduction rules at the end of stack (top):
Consider the last four items: A, B, C, D (D is newly appended or top). A type1 reduction applies if:
- B.active and C.active
- A.value == C.value
- B.value == D.value
Then remove C and D; set A.active = False (if A existed and was active? Actually A absorbs C, so if A was active, length becomes2 -> inactive); set B.active = False (B absorbs D). Score++.
Free deletion (of C) applies if:
- B.active and C.active
- A.value == C.value
- (D absent or B.value != D.value)  [if D present and B.value == D.value, it would be type1]
Then remove C; set A.active = False; B remains active.
Also symmetric free deletion of B? Pattern A,B,C,D with C.value == A.value and B.active,C.active; if D absent or B.value != D.value, we remove C. What about deleting B (the left middle) instead? That would correspond to a different swap (A and B) and might be needed. But perhaps processing left-to-right with the rule above (delete the right occurrence C) is sufficient because if a deletion of the left occurrence is optimal, we could have processed the right occurrence earlier? Need consider.

In the stack algorithm, when we append a new run, we only reduce at the end. This corresponds to performing operations that involve the newly appended run as D or C. It may miss operations that delete a run not at the end. However, any operation in the original sequence can be commuted to happen when the involved runs are at the end of the processed prefix? Not necessarily, but stack algorithms for such reductions often work because reductions only depend on local patterns and can be applied greedily.

Let's test the active stack algorithm on the overcount example V=1,2,1,2,1,3,2,3.

Process with active flags. Initially all active.
Read 1: stack [(1,T)]
Read 2: [(1,T),(2,T)]
Read 1: [(1,T),(2,T),(1,T)]
Read 2: append (2,T). Last4: A=1,B=2,C=1,D=2. B,C active, A==C, B==D -> type1. Remove C,D; A.active=False, B.active=False. Stack becomes [(1,F),(2,F)]. Score1.
Read 1: append (1,T) -> [(1,F),(2,F),(1,T)]
Read 3: append (3,T). Last4: A=1(F),B=2(F),C=1(T),D=3(T). B.active? False, so no type1/free. Stack [(1,F),(2,F),(1,T),(3,T)].
Read 2: append (2,T) -> [(1,F),(2,F),(1,T),(3,T),(2,T)]. Check last4: A=1(T),B=3(T),C=2(T),D=2? Wait last4 are (1,T),(3,T),(2,T),(2,T)? Actually stack values: 1,2,1,3,2. Last4: 2(F),1(T),3(T),2(T). A=2(F),B=1(T),C=3(T),D=2(T). A==C? 2==3 no. Last3: 1(T),3(T),2(T) no equal. No reduction.
Read 3: append (3,T) -> values 1,2,1,3,2,3. Last4: 1(T),3(T),2(T),3(T). A=1,B=3,C=2,D=3: A==C? no. Last4 previous: 2(F),1(T),3(T),2(T)? no. Last3: 3(T),2(T),3(T) has A==C (3==3), B=2 active, D absent? Actually last3 are positions4=3,5=2,6=3; this is pattern a,b,a with a=3,b=2. But D is absent (end). Free deletion of C (last 3) applies if B.active and C.active and A==C and D absent. Here B=2 active, C=3 active, A=3 active -> free deletion: remove C, set A.active=False. Stack becomes 1,2,1,3,2? Wait remove last 3, set A (the first 3 at pos4) inactive. Stack values [1,2,1,3,2] with active flags: 1F,2F,1T,3F,2T. Score still1.
Now no more input. Final score1. This matches our manual max! Good. The active stack algorithm did not overcount because it refused free deletion when B inactive, and at the end it did a free deletion but no type1. So active flags are necessary and sufficient.

Maybe after first type1, instead of stack [1,2], we should represent the reduced sequence as [2,1]? Let's think. The first type1 on runs1-4 [1,2,1,2] produced run sequence [1,2] (runs1,2 merged lengths). But the remaining active runs are runs5-11. The next type1 uses runs2-5 of the original, which in the reduced sequence corresponds to the last run of the reduced prefix (run2=2) plus the next three active runs (1,2,1). So the pattern at the end of the reduced sequence plus new input is [2,1,2,1], not [1,2,1,2]. Why did our stack have [1,2]? Because it kept the whole reduced prefix. The valid pattern starts at the last run of the reduced prefix, not at the first. So when appending new runs, we should consider patterns that start at any position near the end, not just the last four of the entire stack. In the actual sequence, the reduced prefix [1,2] and the new runs [1,2,1,2,...] form a sequence; the type1 pattern can start at the last element of the prefix (2) and include the next three new runs (1,2,1). Thus the stack algorithm should allow the pattern to "cross" the boundary between processed and unprocessed, starting at the top of the stack. So the active stack algorithm with lookahead (check after each append) seems to produce optimal for these examples. Need define reductions carefully and prove/implement.

But is a single greedy stack algorithm always optimal? We need be confident. This is a typical reduction system where applying reductions greedily at the end of the stack yields the maximum number of type1 operations. We can perhaps prove by induction that the stack represents a reduced sequence equivalent to the processed prefix, and any reduction in the future can be applied when the involved runs are at the end. Since we process left to right and only reduce at the end, we might miss reductions that occur entirely within the processed prefix but not at the end. However, if such a reduction existed, it would have been applied when its rightmost run was appended, because at that time it was at the end. Thus the stack is "normal form" with no reducible pattern ending at the last element? But a pattern can start before the last element and end at the last element; we check last4, so yes. What about a pattern that ends before the last element? It would have been reduced earlier. So the stack is irreducible with respect to reductions that end at the current end. This is standard.

Need also consider free deletions that are not at the end? They would have been applied when the deleted run was appended. So okay.

Now, we need implement the stack algorithm and ensure it computes max type1. Let's formalize the reduction rules on the stack.

We process the run-length encoding of A: list of (value, length). Initially each run has length maybe >1. Wait original runs can have length >1. Our active flag for a run is True if length == 1, False if length > 1. Original runs with length >1 are inactive from the start. They cannot be middle B/C, but can be A/D. The stack algorithm should handle initial lengths.

When we append a new run from input, it has length L. active = (L == 1). But if L > 1, it's inactive. However, can a run of length >1 be created by merging and then later be removed as D in a type1? Yes, D can be inactive. In our stack, D is just an item; if it's removed, we pop it. Its length doesn't matter. When A absorbs C, A.active becomes False regardless of previous active? If A was active, length1 + length(C)=2 -> inactive. If A was inactive, stays inactive. So set A.active = False. When B absorbs D, B.active becomes False. If B was active (required), length1 + length(D) -> inactive. So set B.active = False. C must be active. D can be anything.

Free deletion: pattern A,B,C,D (D may be absent? In stack, when we append a run, D is the newly appended run. But free deletion of C can occur when D is the next input; after appending D, the stack has A,B,C,D. If B.value != D.value, remove C. If D is absent (end of input), we might also need to free-delete C when the pattern A,B,C is at the end and no D. In the stack algorithm, after processing all input, there may be a trailing pattern A,B,C with A==C, B.active, C.active; we can free-delete C to reduce runs (free) but it doesn't affect score. It might enable further reductions? Since no more input, free deletion at the end could create a pattern with previous elements? It could reduce the stack and maybe create a type1 entirely within the stack? But if it did, that type1 would end at the new end and should be counted. So after processing all input, we should continue reducing the stack using the same rules with D absent (or sentinel) until no more reductions. This is important for maximizing score? Free deletions don't change score but can create type1s within the stack. For example, if the stack ends with A,B,C and A==C, free-deleting C might make B adjacent to a previous run and create a type1? Let's see. Suppose stack values ... X, A, B, C with A==C, B.active, C.active, and no D. Free deletion removes C, A becomes inactive. The stack becomes ... X, A, B. Could this create a pattern ending at B? Maybe if X==B and A.active? But A is inactive, so not a middle. Could create pattern Y,X,A,B with X==B and A? A is inactive, cannot be middle. So no type1 involving A as middle. But A could be D? Type1 requires middle B,C active; after free deletion, B remains active, A inactive. A type1 could use A as D? D is right outer, not middle; pattern W,X,A,B with X==B? Let's check: last four ... W, X, A, B. For type1, need X.active and A.active as middle, but A inactive. So no. Free deletion at end might not enable type1. But it can reduce final runs without cost, which doesn't affect answer (cost = R0 - score). We only need score, so we can ignore end free deletions? However, free deletions can change the stack and maybe affect score if we later append more input; but at end no more. So after processing all input, we don't need to reduce further for score. But wait a type1 could be formed entirely within the stack that was not reduced earlier because it required D absent? Type1 requires four elements, so it would have been reduced when its last element was appended. So no. Thus we can stop after input; score is final. Free deletions during processing are needed to expose future type1s; we apply them when D is the newly appended run and B.value != D.value. At the end, free deletions don't affect score, so ignore.

But what about a free deletion where D is absent and it would expose a type1 with future input? Future input doesn't exist. So ignore.

Now, when appending a new run, we should first append it, then check for reductions at the end. There are two possible reductions:
1. Type1 on last4: A,B,C,D with B.active, C.active, A.value == C.value, B.value == D.value. Apply: pop C and D? Actually remove the last two elements (C and D) from the stack, and set A.active = False, B.active = False. Score++.
2. Free deletion on last4: A,B,C,D with B.active, C.active, A.value == C.value, B.value != D.value. Apply: remove C (the third from end), set A.active = False. B remains active. D remains.
But if both conditions? Type1 has priority (B.value == D.value). If B.value != D.value, free deletion. What if A.value != C.value? No reduction.

After applying a reduction, the stack length decreases, and new patterns may appear at the end. We should loop until no reduction applies to the last4 (or last3 for free deletion with D absent? not needed during input because D is the newly appended run; after a reduction, the new D is some existing element, and free deletion with D absent not relevant. But after a type1, the last element of the stack could be part of a pattern with the previous three; we check again.)

Need be careful: The free deletion rule as stated removes C, but what about the case where D is the newly appended run and B.value == D.value? That's type1, which removes C and D. If B.value != D.value, free deletion removes C. This matches the actual operation: pattern A,B,C,D with A==C. If B==D, swapping B,C merges both pairs (type1). If B!=D, swapping B,C merges A,C only, leaving B,D separate (free). Good.

What about the symmetric case where the pattern is A,B,C,D with C.value == A.value but we might instead delete B (the left middle) if B has an equal run two positions to the right? That would be pattern A,B,C,D with B.value == D.value and A.value != C.value? Let's derive symmetric free deletion: Suppose pattern A,B,C,D with B.value == D.value and A.value != C.value. This is like a,x,y,x? Actually B==D, A!=C. Swapping B,C would merge B and D? Original A,B,C,D; swap B,C -> A,C,B,D. If B==D, then B and D merge, so B is removed? Let's see pattern A=1, B=2, C=3, D=2. Swap B,C -> 1,3,2,2 -> runs 1,3,2. This deletes B? Original runs 1,2,3,2; after swap 1,3,2, R decreases by1 (free). This is the symmetric free deletion where the left middle B is deleted (merged with D), not C. Our rule only deletes C when A==C. But we might need to delete B in some cases. In the example 1 2 1 3 2 3, the free deletion was A==C (1==1). But there could be cases where only B==D and A!=C, and deleting B is beneficial. Does our stack algorithm need to handle both directions? Since we process left to right, when we append D, the pattern A,B,C,D with B==D and A!=C is a free deletion of B (the run before C). This operation would remove B and merge it with D, leaving A,C,D? Let's simulate: A=1,B=2,C=3,D=2. Swap B,C -> A,C,B,D = 1,3,2,2 -> runs 1,3,2. So B is removed, C remains, D merged with B. This is a valid free operation. Could it be necessary for optimal? We need include it.

Similarly, type1 requires both A==C and B==D. If only B==D, free deletion of B. If only A==C, free deletion of C. If neither, no free deletion. If both, type1 (better). So the general reduction on last4 A,B,C,D:
- If B.active and C.active:
  - If A.value == C.value and B.value == D.value: type1 (remove C,D, score++, A.active=False, B.active=False)
  - Else if A.value == C.value: free delete C (remove C, A.active=False)
  - Else if B.value == D.value: free delete B (remove B, D.active? D absorbs B, so D.active=False? Wait B is active length1, D may have length; after merging, D length increases, so D.active=False. B removed. C remains. The order becomes A,C,D? Let's see after removing B from A,B,C,D, we get A,C,D. But D's value equals B, and D absorbs B, so D.active=False. Is C adjacent to A? A and C may have same value? If A==C, that would be type1, already handled. So A!=C. Good.)
  - Else: no reduction.
But wait if A.value == C.value and B.value == D.value, type1 removes C,D and sets A,B inactive. If we instead did free delete C then free delete B? Would that yield same score? Type1 gives score1; two free deletions would give score0 but same run reduction? Let's check pattern A,B,C,D with A==C, B==D. Free delete C -> A,B,D (D==B, so B and D adjacent? Actually after removing C, sequence A,B,D with B==D, they would merge, so run count would reduce by2? This is type1 but if we treat as free delete C, we should then merge B,D, which is effectively type1. So type1 is the correct combined operation with score1. We shouldn't do free delete C then another free delete B because after removing C, B and D are adjacent equal and would merge automatically, reducing runs by an extra 1 without a swap? Wait merging adjacent equal runs is not an operation; it's just how run-length encoding collapses. In the actual swap, both merges happen in one swap. If we conceptually remove C, the sequence A,B,D has B and D adjacent equal, so they would be one run, meaning D as a separate run disappears. That's exactly type1. So we should treat it as type1 with score1, not two free deletions.

Similarly, if A==C but B==D, our type1 rule handles.

Now, what about free deletion of B when B.active and C.active and B.value == D.value, A.value != C.value. After removing B, D absorbs B, D.active=False. C remains. Does C need to be active? Yes, because we swap B and C; both middle runs must be singleton for Δ=-1. So C.active required. A can be anything.

What about free deletion of C when A==C, B.active, C.active, B!=D. After removing C, A absorbs C, A.active=False. B remains active. D remains.

These two free deletion directions correspond to deleting the left or right of the two middle runs. Our stack algorithm should apply either when possible. Which one to choose if both? If both A==C and B==D, type1. If only one, that one. If both A==C and B==D impossible to have only one. So deterministic.

But can both free deletions be possible in different patterns overlapping? The stack reduction loop will handle.

Now, we need prove that greedily applying these reductions at the end of the stack maximizes score. This is a typical "stack reduction" for a confluent rewriting system. We can perhaps trust but let's test more examples.

Let's manually test a tricky sequence: V = 1 2 3 2 3. Runs R=5. What is optimal? Stack:
Read 1,2,3,2,3.
After 1,2,3,2: S=[1,2,3,2]. Last4 A=1,B=2,C=3,D=2: B==D (2==2), A!=C -> free delete B. Remove B, D.active=False. S becomes [1,3,2]? Wait remove B (second element) from [1,2,3,2] -> [1,3,2], and D (last 2) absorbs B, so D.active=False. But D is the last element, value2. S values [1,3,2] with active: 1T,3T,2F? Actually D was active before, now inactive. Score0.
Read 3: append 3T -> [1T,3T,2F,3T]. Last4 A=1,B=3,C=2,D=3: A!=C, B==D? 3==3 yes -> free delete B. Remove B (3), D absorbs B. S becomes [1T,2F,3F]? Wait remove second element (3) from [1,3,2,3] -> [1,2,3]; D (last 3) absorbs B, so D.active=False. S=[1T,2F,3F]. Score0. Final score0, cost R=5. Is that optimal? Original runs 1,2,3,2,3. Can we do type1? Pattern 2,3,2,3 at runs2-5: A=2,B=3,C=2,D=3, middle B,C active, A==C, B==D -> type1! Wait our stack algorithm missed it because it reduced too early. Let's simulate correctly:
V=1,2,3,2,3. At the end, last4 are 2,3,2,3 (runs2-5), not 1,2,3,2. Our stack after reading 1,2,3,2 was [1,2,3,2]; we applied free delete B because B==D (2==2) with pattern 1,2,3,2. But in the actual sequence, we should not delete B yet; we should wait for the next 3 to form type1 on runs2-5. The stack algorithm with immediate free deletion was too greedy and destroyed the type1! This is a counterexample to the greedy stack as described. Let's verify actual optimal for V=1,2,3,2,3:
A runs all singleton: 1 2 3 2 3. Type1 on runs2-5 (2,3,2,3): swap positions3? Original 1,2,3,2,3. Swap pos3(3) and pos4(2): 1,2,2,3,3. Runs [1,2,3] R=3. Cost =1 swap +3 deletions =4. Initial R=5, score1. So answer should be4. Our stack got score0, wrong. Thus we cannot apply free deletion immediately if it prevents a future type1. Need lookahead or different algorithm.

The issue: Pattern A,B,C,D with B==D and A!=C is a free deletion of B, but if the next input E equals C, then the original pattern B,C,D,E (where B==D, C==E) is a type1 that should take priority. In V=1,2,3,2,3, after reading 1,2,3,2, the next is 3. The type1 on runs2-5 uses B=2,C=3,D=2,E=3. The free deletion on runs1-4 (1,2,3,2) would delete B=2, preventing type1. Since type1 gives saving1 and free deletion gives0, we should prefer the type1 when possible. But at the time we read 2 (D), we didn't know next is 3. A stack algorithm with one-step lookahead could delay free deletion until we see next input. In general, we should not perform a free deletion if the next input could complete a type1. Since we process online, we can look at the next run before deciding reductions? Or we can maintain the stack and only reduce when the reduction is "forced" (i.e., no future input can change it). This is like the classic greedy for removing duplicates: delay reductions until the end.

Maybe we should process the entire run sequence and then reduce using a stack with backtracking? Or use dynamic programming.

Let's analyze the rewriting system more carefully. The free deletion of B in pattern A,B,C,D with B==D is optional and can be harmful. We need choose reductions to maximize type1. This is a context-free optimization.

Maybe we can model it as a DP on the run sequence with states representing the stack. Since R up to 2e5, perhaps a stack with "postponement" can work: only apply type1 reductions when possible; never apply free deletions? But free deletions were necessary in example 1 2 1 3 2 3 to get a type1. There, free deletion of C (A==C) was necessary. Could we postpone it until after seeing more? In that example, after reading 1,2,1,3, the next is 2. The free deletion of C (1) with pattern 1,2,1,3; if we postpone, the sequence 1,2,1,3,2,3 has a type1? Original 1,2,1,3,2,3 had no type1 initially, so free deletion was needed to create one. How to decide? We need know whether a free deletion enables a future type1.

This is analogous to parsing with rewrite rules:
- Type1: a b a b → a b (score +1)
- Free: a b a c → a b c (if c != b) [delete second a]
- Free: a b c b → a c b (if a != c) [delete first b? Actually pattern a,b,c,b -> a,c,b]
These rules are length-reducing. We want max number of type1 applications. This is a string rewriting problem. It might be confluent if we prioritize type1? Let's test. In V=1,2,3,2,3, if we prioritize type1 over free, we get score1. In V=1,2,1,3,2,3, no type1 initially; free deletion of second 1 (rule a b a c) leads to 1,2,3,2,3, then type1 on 2,3,2,3. So free deletion is needed.

Maybe the optimal strategy is: At each step, if a type1 reduction exists anywhere, apply it (perhaps leftmost/rightmost). If no type1, apply a free deletion that leads to a type1. This is like reducing the string by removing "bad" patterns.

Could there be a polynomial DP using intervals? Since R up to 2e5, maybe the run sequence has special property: values are from 1..N, but no adjacent equal. The rewrite rules are local. We can compute maximum type1 via dynamic programming on the stack using the concept of "minimum final stack length" with scores. This is similar to computing the result of a deterministic pushdown automaton if the system is confluent. Is it confluent? Let's test with V=1,2,3,2,3: applying free deletion first gives score0, type1 first gives score1. Not confluent. So need optimize.

Maybe the problem can be solved by dynamic programming on the run sequence where we decide for each run whether it is "deleted" or "kept", and type1 score corresponds to something like matching adjacent kept runs. Let's think in terms of the final run sequence after all swaps (before deletions). The cost = swaps + final runs. We want minimize. The swaps are adjacent swaps; each swap can reduce runs by at most2. The final run sequence is obtained from the initial run sequence by some sequence of swaps. This is equivalent to choosing a final sequence B (stable permutation) with runs. The number of swaps is inversions. Maybe we can directly compute min over final run sequences using DP on the run values? The final run sequence is a subsequence? Actually swaps can change the order of runs, not just delete them. But in our run-reduction model, operations either remove runs (free/type1) or keep them; they don't reorder runs except moving a singleton across another. The final run sequence is a subsequence of the original run sequence? Let's check:
- Type1 on A,B,C,D -> A,B (C,D removed). Final A,B are original A,B. So final is a subsequence (A,B) of original (A,B,C,D).
- Free deletion of C in A,B,C,D -> A,B,D. This is original with C removed; order A,B,D preserved. So final is a subsequence.
- Free deletion of B in A,B,C,D -> A,C,D. This is original with B removed; order A,C,D preserved.
Thus every operation removes one or two runs and leaves the order of remaining runs unchanged! Is that always true? Adjacent swaps of singleton runs can change order, but the net effect on run sequence is deletion of runs, not reordering? Let's verify type1 A,B,C,D -> A,B (C,D removed), order A,B same. Free deletion C -> A,B,D (C removed), order A,B,D same. Free deletion B -> A,C,D (B removed), order A,C,D same. What about a swap that doesn't reduce runs (Δ=0) would reorder runs, but we argued it's not beneficial? Maybe optimal can avoid Δ=0. If we only use Δ=-2 and Δ=-1, the run sequence after operations is always a subsequence of the original run sequence! This is a huge insight. Let's prove: Any swap between two singleton runs that results in Δ=-2 or Δ=-1 removes one or two runs and preserves the relative order of the remaining runs. Yes, because the swapped singleton runs either merge with neighbors or one remains; the sequence of run values after merging is the original sequence with the merged runs removed. No reordering of remaining runs. Therefore the final run sequence (after all beneficial swaps) is a subsequence of the original run sequence. Moreover, the runs removed are exactly those consumed by free/type1 operations. The number of type1 operations is the number of times we remove two runs at once (with score).

Thus the problem reduces to: Choose a subsequence of the original run sequence to keep (the final runs). The removed runs can be partitioned into groups:
- Single removals (free): remove one run, cost0.
- Double removals (type1): remove two adjacent runs, score+1.
The kept runs remain in original order. The condition for a group of removed runs to be valid is local based on the kept runs around them:
- A single removed run C can be removed if it has the same value as the kept run two positions to its left? Wait in the subsequence after previous removals, the neighbors of C in the current sequence are some kept/not-yet-removed runs. The free deletion of C requires in the current sequence pattern A,B,C with A.value == C.value and B active. Since B is a singleton run that is not removed? Actually B remains (in free deletion of C, B remains). So B is a kept run (or will be removed later? It remains at that moment; could be removed later). The run A remains and absorbs C, so A is kept. Thus a single removal of C is valid if in the current sequence, the run immediately before C (B) and the run two before (A) exist, A.value == C.value, and B is active (singleton). B must be active at that time, meaning B has not been involved in a previous merge that made it inactive. If B is eventually kept, it could be active if its length remained1. If B was previously used as A in a removal, it would be inactive. So active status depends on whether a run has absorbed a removed run to its left/right.

This is getting complicated but maybe can be modeled as a DP over the run sequence with states indicating whether each run is active. However, since final sequence is a subsequence, we can think of each original run as either:
- Kept and active (length1) in final? Actually if a kept run absorbs removed runs, its length increases, so it becomes inactive. In the final run sequence, all runs have some length; active status only matters for future removals. A run that absorbs a removed run becomes inactive and cannot be the middle B in future removals, but can be A/D.

This is similar to the "active stack" but with delayed decisions. Maybe we can compute maximum type1 using dynamic programming on the run sequence with a stack of active runs? Let's consider the final subsequence. Removed runs are paired/deleted. The active status of a kept run is False if it has absorbed any removed run, True otherwise. A run can absorb a removed run of the same value on its left or right? In free deletion of C with A==C, A absorbs C (right side). In free deletion of B with B==D, D absorbs B (left side? B is before D, D absorbs B). In type1, A absorbs C, B absorbs D. So a kept run can absorb a removed run of the same value adjacent to it after previous removals. This is like matching removed runs to kept runs of the same value.

Maybe we can view each type1 as deleting two adjacent runs C,D and incrementing the "inactive" status of the two preceding runs A,B. This is analogous to a reduction where we match a pair of adjacent runs with the two runs immediately before them if values match. This is exactly the rewrite rule:
A B C D -> A B  (if A=C, B=D, and B,C active)
where A,B remain but become inactive.
Free deletion:
A B C D -> A B D (if A=C, B!=D) [C removed, A inactive]
A B C D -> A C D (if B=D, A!=C) [B removed, D inactive]

This is a string rewriting system on a sequence where each character has a value and an active flag. Initially all active. We want max number of applications of the first rule (score). This is similar to reducing a word by a context-free grammar. Could be solved by dynamic programming with a stack if we process from left to right and maintain a canonical "irreducible" form, but the counterexample showed naive greedy free deletion fails. However, maybe if we prioritize type1 over free deletion and use a stack with backtracking (like shift-reduce with lookahead), we can get optimal. In V=1,2,3,2,3, at stack [1,2,3,2], the free deletion of B (2) was possible, but the next input 3 would create type1. A shift-reduce parser with lookahead of 1 would see that after shifting 3, a type1 reduction is possible, so it should shift instead of reduce. This is exactly LR parsing. The grammar might be deterministic with one-token lookahead. We can implement a greedy algorithm: scan left to right, maintain stack; at each step, decide whether to reduce now or shift next run based on whether a type1 reduction will be possible after shifting. This is complex but possible.

Maybe there is a simpler dynamic programming: Since each type1 removes two adjacent runs and the two preceding runs remain, we can think of type1 as matching a pattern of 4 runs where the middle two are active. This is like finding a maximum matching in a path with constraints, which can be solved by DP O(R). Let's explore.

Consider the original run sequence (values, lengths initially all 1). A type1 operation on runs i-2, i-1, i, i+1 (where i-1 and i are active) removes runs i and i+1, and inactivates runs i-2 and i-1. After this, runs i-2 and i-1 remain but inactive; runs i and i+1 gone. This is similar to matching runs i and i+1 as a "pair" that is deleted, with the condition that the two runs immediately before them (i-2, i-1) have values matching (i, i+1) respectively. The runs i-2 and i-1 become "used" (inactive) and cannot be the middle of another type1, but can be the left part of another type1? They can be A/B in a future type1 if the pattern starts at i-2? Maybe we can solve the problem using dynamic programming on the run sequence with the following state: the current "stack" of runs that are kept and active? Let's consider a different approach: Since N total 2e5, maybe we can use dynamic programming with a segment tree over values for the label assignment problem. Let's revisit label model with the subsequence insight.

The final run sequence is a subsequence of the original run sequence. The number of type1 operations equals (initial R - final R - free_deletions)/2? Actually final R = R0 - 2t - f. Cost = R0 - t. We don't need f. The final run sequence is some subsequence. The swaps cost = t + f. The total cost = final R + t + f = R0 - t. So minimizing cost is maximizing t. What is t in terms of final subsequence and removed runs? Each type1 removes two runs; each free removes one. The removed runs are partitioned into t pairs and f singles. A pair removed by type1 consists of two adjacent runs in the current sequence at the time of removal, with the two preceding runs matching their values. This is like a reduction where we can delete pairs of adjacent runs if the two runs immediately before them (in the current sequence) have the same values. This is exactly the rule of a "stack" where kept runs act as a stack and removed pairs are matched with the top two stack elements.

This is analogous to matching parentheses: We scan runs; a type1 occurs when the next two runs match the top two runs of the stack in an alternating way. Specifically, if the stack top two are (a,b) and the next two input runs are (a,b), then we can delete the next two and score1, leaving the stack (a,b) but marking them inactive. This is like the stack top two "absorb" the next two. In alternating, the stack top two (1,2) absorb the next (1,2), score1, but the stack remains (1,2) (now inactive). Then the next input (1,2) can be absorbed again? Wait after absorbing (1,2), the stack top two are the same values (1,2) but inactive. The next input (1,2) would match top two values (1,2), but the middle runs for the type1 would be the top two? In the actual sequence, after first absorption, the stack top two are inactive, so they cannot be the middle B,C; but they can be A,B? Let's map: Stack top two (1,2) inactive. Next input (1,2). The pattern for type1 is A=2? Wait if stack top two are (1,2) and next two are (1,2), the four values are 1,2,1,2. The middle two are stack top (2) and first input (1). The stack top (2) is inactive, so invalid. But actual second type1 used pattern 2,1,2,1, not 1,2,1,2. So the stack top two after first absorption should perhaps be considered as (2,1) instead of (1,2)? Let's see after first type1 on original 1,2,1,2, the remaining kept runs are original run1=1 and run2=2, in that order. Let's not get stuck. Perhaps there is a known solution using dynamic programming on the run sequence with "maximum score = maximum number of disjoint pairs of equal adjacent runs in a stack" that can be computed by a greedy algorithm with a stack of active runs and no free deletions? Let's try to solve the label assignment problem with dynamic programming over the run sequence using the fact that final sequence is a subsequence. We can define DP[i] = maximum score for prefix up to run i, with some state about the last two runs in the reduced sequence and their active status. Since values can be many, but the last two values matter for future type1. This is like a finite-state automaton if we only need last two values? However, free deletions can remove runs, so the "last two runs" of the reduced prefix can be any pair of original runs. We can maintain a stack of possible states? Maybe we can use a segment tree to compute the best score for each possible last value.

Let's define the reduction process on the run sequence. At any point, the reduced sequence is a subsequence of the processed prefix. Future operations only involve the end of this reduced sequence (since we process left to right and reductions are local at the end). Is that true? In the actual sequence, a type1 can occur in the middle of the remaining sequence, not necessarily at the end of the processed prefix. But if we process left to right, when we reach the rightmost run of a type1, it is at the end of the processed prefix, so we can apply it. Thus any operation can be applied at the moment its rightmost run is processed. Therefore the reduced sequence for the prefix is the current sequence after all operations whose rightmost run has been processed. Operations that would occur later involve future runs. So the state is exactly the reduced sequence of the processed prefix. Future operations only need the suffix of this reduced sequence (because a type1 involving future runs will have its A,B in the reduced prefix and C,D in future, so it depends on the last few runs of the reduced prefix). Thus we can maintain the reduced prefix as a stack; when a new run arrives, we decide whether to reduce at the end. This is the stack approach. The counterexample showed that deciding free deletion too early is bad; but if we delay free deletion until we know the next input, we can make the right choice. In a standard stack parser, we can look ahead at the next input before reducing. Since we have the entire sequence, we can implement a shift-reduce parser with lookahead of 1 (or more) to avoid reducing when the next input would create a type1.

Let's design a deterministic shift-reduce algorithm:
- Stack S represents reduced prefix.
- We have a queue of remaining input runs.
- At each step, we can either shift the next input run onto S, or reduce the top of S using a rule.
We want maximize score. This is like a parsing problem. The grammar:
Type1 reduction: S ... A B C D -> S ... A B (score+1), if A=C, B=D, B,C active.
Free reductions:
S ... A B C D -> S ... A B D (if A=C, B!=D) [remove C]
S ... A B C D -> S ... A C D (if B=D, A!=C) [remove B]
We can choose reductions. This is exactly a context-free grammar with productions:
A B A B → A B (score)
A B A C → A B C
A B C B → A C B
where B,C must be active (but active flags update).

This grammar might be unambiguous if we use a greedy strategy: shift until a type1 reduction is possible at the top, then reduce; if no type1 possible, shift more? In V=1,2,3,2,3, if we shift all, top has type1 2,3,2,3, reduce. In V=1,2,1,3,2,3, after shifting all, top? Stack 1,2,1,3,2,3. Top4 1,3,2,3 no; top4 2,1,3,2 no; top4 1,2,1,3 no. No type1. Maybe left-to-right with "apply type1 at end whenever possible; if no type1 and end of input, apply free deletion at end" works? So a correct algorithm needs lookahead to decide whether to apply a free deletion. Specifically, when top4 has a free deletion and no type1, we should check if shifting the next input would create a type1 at the top. If yes, shift instead of free-delete. If no, apply free deletion? So to maintain top-only reductions, we should apply free deletion before shifting if it enables a type1 at the top. How to know? We can look ahead: after free deletion, would the top4 become a type1? Maintain stack S.

  If top4 of S is a type1, apply it (score++), continue.

Maybe the optimal score can be achieved by repeatedly:
1. Apply any type1 at the top of the stack (after shifting all? or left-to-right).

Given time, perhaps we can implement the greedy algorithm:
- Process runs left to right.
- Maintain stack of (value, active).
- For each new run, push it.
- Then, in a loop:
   - If top4 forms a valid type1, apply it, score++, continue.
   - Else if top4 allows a free deletion AND applying it would make the new top4 a valid type1 (or maybe top3? type1 requires top4), then apply the free deletion and continue.
   - Else break.
This algorithm is deterministic and handles examples. But is it sufficient for all cases? We need be confident.

Now, implement the algorithm on the run-length encoding of A.

Data structures:
- We need run list: values and lengths. But we can process original A and build runs on the fly? The algorithm needs lookahead? It processes runs one by one; no lookahead beyond current run? Actually the algorithm as described shifts next run and then reduces; no explicit lookahead except the "free deletion only if immediate type1" condition which is evaluated on the current stack after a free deletion, not on future input. It doesn't need lookahead! Wait in V=1,2,3,2,3, after reading 1,2,3,2, top4 has free deletion but not immediate type1, so algorithm does nothing and shifts next 3. This is just "do not apply free deletion unless it immediately creates type1". No lookahead needed. Good.

So algorithm:
Initialize stack = [] of [value, active].
score = 0.
For each run (value, length) in RLE:
  active = (length == 1)
  stack.append([value, active])
  while True:
    if len(stack) >= 4:
      A=stack[-4], B=stack[-3], C=stack[-2], D=stack[-1]
      if B.active and C.active and A.value == C.value and B.value == D.value:
         # type1
         # remove C and D: pop last two
         stack.pop(); stack.pop()
         # A and B become inactive
         stack[-2].active = False  # A (now at -2 after pop? Let's check)
         stack[-1].active = False  # B
         score += 1
         continue
      # free deletion that immediately creates type1?
      # We need check if applying a free deletion would make the new top4 a type1.
      # There are two free deletion variants:
      # Variant C: A.value == C.value and B.value != D.value, B.active, C.active -> remove C, A inactive.
      # Variant B: B.value == D.value and A.value != C.value, B.active, C.active -> remove B, D inactive.
      # We should apply a variant only if after removal, the new top4 (or maybe top3? type1 needs 4) is a valid type1.
      # But what if after removal, the stack length <4 and no type1; then not immediate.
      # Let's implement a helper to simulate removal and check.
    else:
      break
But we must be careful: After a free deletion, the active flags change. We need simulate both variants and see if the resulting stack's top4 is a type1. If yes, apply that free deletion and continue (then the type1 will be applied in next loop). If both variants lead to type1? Choose one; either should be fine? Could both be possible? If A==C and B==D, it's type1 handled. If A==C only, variant C; if B==D only, variant B. Both cannot be true unless type1. So at most one variant.

However, what if a free deletion immediately creates a type1 not at the top4 but at top3? Type1 requires 4, so no.

What if after free deletion, the stack length is exactly 4 and top4 is type1; we apply. Good.

But is it enough to only apply free deletion if it immediately creates type1? What if free deletion immediately creates another free deletion that then creates type1? The algorithm would not apply because immediate type1 false. Could that be necessary? Example: free deletion A==C removes C, resulting in pattern X,A,B,D where maybe another free deletion then type1. If we don't apply, maybe miss. Let's try to construct. Suppose stack top4 A,B,C,D with A==C, B!=D. Remove C -> stack ... A,B,D. For this to lead to type1 after another free deletion, the new top4 might have a free deletion. But if we don't apply the first free deletion, we shift next run; maybe the opportunity is lost. Our algorithm would not apply first free deletion because immediate type1 false. Is there a case where this is suboptimal? Let's search.

We need stack S = ... X, A, B, C, D with A==C, B!=D, and after removing C, the new stack ... X,A,B,D has a free deletion that, when applied, creates a type1. For example, new top4 Y,X,A,B maybe with X==B? Let's set values. Let S before: X, A, B, C, D. A==C. Remove C -> X,A,B,D. Suppose top4 X,A,B,D has free deletion of A? Pattern X,A,B,D with X==B? Free deletion of A? Wait free deletion of A (left middle) requires A? Let's use variants on X,A,B,D:
- Variant C: X.value == B.value and A.active? Actually pattern X,A,B,D: to delete B (the third) need X==B and A.active? Let's recall free deletion of C in pattern A,B,C,D requires A==C, B.active, C.active. So in X,A,B,D, to delete B (third), need X.value == B.value, A.active, B.active. A is inactive? A absorbed C in first free deletion, so A.active=False. Thus cannot delete B. To delete A (second), need B.value == D.value and A.active? Pattern X,A,B,D, free deletion of A (left middle) requires A.active? Actually variant B deletes B (the second run) when B.value == D.value and A.active? Let's define: pattern P,Q,R,S. Free deletion of Q (second) if Q.value == S.value and P.value != R.value, and Q.active, R.active. So in X,A,B,D, to delete A (second), need A.value == D.value and X.value != B.value, A.active, B.active. A is inactive, so no. Thus first free deletion inactivates A, preventing further free deletions involving A as middle. So immediate chain unlikely.

What about variant B free deletion (remove B) inactivates D, not B? In pattern A,B,C,D with B==D, remove B, D inactive. The remaining A,C,D; B removed. A remains active? In variant B, B is removed, D absorbs B, C remains, A remains active (unless A was inactive). So A could be middle later. If after removing B, new top4 ... X,A,C,D might have a free deletion. But A active, C active. Could lead to type1. Our algorithm would only apply variant B if immediate type1 after removal. Is there a case where variant B not immediate but necessary? Let's try to construct.

S = X, A, B, C, D with B==D, A!=C. Remove B -> X,A,C,D (D inactive). For immediate type1 in X,A,C,D, need pattern X,A,C,D with A.active, C.active, X==C, A==D. Since D inactive but D can be D in type1 (right outer) no active requirement. So if X==C and A==D, then type1. If not, no. Could another free deletion on X,A,C,D create type1? To delete A (second), need A.value == D.value and X.value != C.value, A.active, C.active. If A==D, then that's actually immediate type1 if X==C? Wait if A==D and X==C, immediate type1. If A==D but X!=C, then free deletion of A (variant B) would remove A, D inactive, leaving X,C,D. Could this then create type1? Maybe with future runs. But if we don't apply first free deletion, we shift future runs; maybe still okay.

Given the complexity, perhaps the intended solution is not this greedy but a simpler DP. Let's search memory again. The problem might be from AtCoder "AGC 063 B" with solution using "run-length encoding and stack; answer = len(runs) - number of times stack top 3 form a pattern"? Maybe the official solution uses a stack that reduces whenever top 3 values satisfy something, and counts. Let's think of a different invariant.

Maybe the maximum number of type1 operations equals the maximum number of disjoint pairs of adjacent runs in the original run sequence that can be removed by the rewrite rule, which can be computed by a simple stack of values ignoring active flags if we process from right to left? Let's test V=1,2,3,2,3. If we process from right to left with value-only stack:
Input reversed: 3,2,3,2,1.
Stack: 3,2,3,2 -> pattern 3,2,3,2 type1 -> reduce to 3,2, score1. Then 1 -> 3,2,1. Score1. Good.
V=1,2,1,3,2,3 reversed: 3,2,3,1,2,1.
Stack: 3,2,3,1 -> no; 2 -> 3,2,3,1,2; 1 -> 3,2,3,1,2,1. Top4 1,2,1? Actually last4 3,1,2,1 no; 2,3,1,2 no; 3,2,3,1 no. No type1. Free deletion? Value-only stack maybe no. But optimal score1. So right-to-left value-only fails.

What if we use a stack and whenever top3 are a,b,a, we pop the middle? No.

Let's look at the problem constraints: T up to 1e5, sum N 2e5. The intended solution likely O(N log N) or O(N). The run-reduction game with active flags and greedy might be the intended, but we need be certain.

Let's try to prove the greedy algorithm:
We process runs left to right. The stack is the reduced sequence after performing all operations that are "forced" by the prefix. A type1 reduction at the top is always safe because it gives +1 score and cannot reduce the maximum possible future score? Need show. If a type1 is available at the top, applying it is optimal. This is plausible because type1s are the only source of score and they don't consume future runs; they consume current top runs. Could applying a type1 at the top prevent a later type1 that would give more score? The later type1 would require the runs consumed by this type1. Since those runs are at the top and would otherwise remain, maybe they could be used differently. But in examples, applying leftmost/available type1 was optimal. We need a formal exchange argument: A type1 at the top uses runs A,B,C,D and leaves A,B inactive. If we don't apply it, C,D remain active and could be used as middle runs in future type1s. Could that yield more than 1 score? For example, in alternating, if we don't apply the first type1, we could apply a type1 on runs2-5 instead, then runs1-4? Let's see original 1,2,1,2,1,2,1. If we skip first type1, type1 on runs2-5 (2,1,2,1) gives score1, then maybe type1 on runs? Sequence after type1 on runs2-5: 1,2,1,2,1? Actually original 1,2,1,2,1,2,1; swap runs3,4 -> 1,2,2,1,1,2,1? Runs [1,2,1,2,1]? Then type1 on runs? Maybe score2. Applying first type1 also led to score2? For length7, formula floor(6/3)=2. Both okay. So type1 at top doesn't reduce max.

Free deletion only if it immediately creates type1: A free deletion gives no score, so we should only do it if it enables a type1 that would otherwise be missed. If it immediately creates a type1 at the top, we can do it and get +1. If it doesn't, doing it now vs later (or not) cannot increase score because it just removes a run; the run might be needed for a future type1. So postponing is safe. This is a reasonable greedy.

Let's implement this greedy and hope.

Need define "immediately creates a type1" after free deletion. We can simulate the free deletion on a copy of the last up to 4 elements, but active flags of A/B/D change. Since we only need check top4 after removal, we can write a helper `would_create_type1_after_free(stack, variant)`.

But maybe we can simplify: Instead of checking immediate type1, we can always postpone free deletions until the end of input, and then apply free deletions that create type1. However, as seen, a free deletion before the end may be needed to expose a type1 before later runs are shifted, but if we postpone, the type1 might be buried. Our algorithm applies free deletion only when it immediately creates type1; this handles that.

Let's implement the stack as lists of [value, active]. We'll process RLE.

Pseudo:

def solve_case(A):
    # build runs
    runs = []
    for x in A:
        if runs and runs[-1][0] == x:
            runs[-1][1] += 1
        else:
            runs.append([x, 1])
    stack = []  # each [value, active]
    score = 0

    def is_type1(S):
        if len(S) < 4: return False
        A,B,C,D = S[-4],S[-3],S[-2],S[-1]
        return B[1] and C[1] and A[0] == C[0] and B[0] == D[0]

    def apply_type1(S):
        nonlocal score
        # S length >=4
        S.pop(); S.pop()
        S[-2][1] = False
        S[-1][1] = False
        score += 1

    for val, length in runs:
        stack.append([val, length == 1])
        while True:
            changed = False
            if is_type1(stack):
                apply_type1(stack)
                changed = True
                continue
            # try free deletions that immediately lead to type1
            if len(stack) >= 4:
                A,B,C,D = stack[-4],stack[-3],stack[-2],stack[-1]
                # variant C: remove C if A.val == C.val and B.active and C.active and B.val != D.val
                if B[1] and C[1] and A[0] == C[0] and B[0] != D[0]:
                    # simulate removal of C
                    # new stack: ... A, B, D (A inactive)
                    # We need check if new stack top4 is type1.
                    # Since only top changed, we can create a temporary list of last up to 5? Simpler: copy stack? Stack size up to N, copying each time too slow.
                    # We can check by constructing the new top4 without full copy.
                    # After removing C, the last three are A, B, D (if no elements after D? D was last, so yes).
                    # New top4 consists of (element before A), A, B, D.
                    # We need check if len(stack) >=5? Actually after removal, stack length = len(stack)-1. Top4 are stack[-5] (if exists), A, B, D.
                    # Let's write a helper that takes the current stack and indices of the new last 4 after removal.
                    pass

Given time, maybe we can just copy the last 5 elements for simulation; O(1) per reduction. Since each run is pushed once and each reduction pops at least one, total O(N). Copying last 5 is fine.

We'll implement a function `check_type1_on_list(last4)` that takes four items and returns bool. For simulation:
- Variant C removal of C (stack[-2]):
   New last4 (if len(stack) >=5 after removal? Actually after removal, stack length = len(stack)-1. The new last4 are:
     if len(stack) == 4: after removal length3, no type1.
     else: elements at positions len(stack)-5, len(stack)-4, len(stack)-3, len(stack)-2? Let's use original indices.
Original stack indices: ..., X = stack[-5] (if exists), A=stack[-4], B=stack[-3], C=stack[-2], D=stack[-1].
Remove C -> ..., X, A, B, D.
New last4: if X exists, [X,A,B,D]; else no.
We need check type1 on [X,A,B,D] with the active flags after update: A.active becomes False; B.active remains; D.active remains. X unchanged.
- Variant B removal of B (stack[-3]):
   Remove B -> ..., X, A, C, D (D.active becomes False).
   New last4: if X exists, [X,A,C,D]; A.active remains, C.active remains, D.active=False.
But wait after removing B, the order is A,C,D? Original ... X,A,B,C,D. Remove B -> ... X,A,C,D. Yes.
We should also consider variant C where D absent? Not during processing because D is the newly appended run; stack length >=4. At the end, we don't need free deletions for score.

If after simulation the new top4 is type1, we apply the actual free deletion:
- Variant C: pop stack[-2] (C), set stack[-2].active = False (A). Note after popping C, A is at index -2? Original A=stack[-4], after pop C, stack[-2] is A? Let's check: stack [..., A, B, C, D]; pop C -> [..., A, B, D]; stack[-2]=A, stack[-1]=B? Actually D is last, B is second last, A third last. So set stack[-3].active? Let's use indices after pop: A = stack[-3], B = stack[-2], D = stack[-1]. So set stack[-3].active = False. B remains active.
- Variant B: pop stack[-3] (B). Original [..., A,B,C,D]; pop index -3 -> [..., A,C,D]. After pop, A=stack[-3], C=stack[-2], D=stack[-1]. Set stack[-1].active = False (D absorbs B). A remains active.
Then continue loop.

But wait in variant C, if A.active was already False, setting False okay. In variant B, D.active becomes False.

What about free deletion of C when B.value == D.value? That's type1, already handled. Free deletion of B when A.value == C.value? Type1, handled.

Now, after applying a free deletion, the loop continues; it may apply type1 or another free deletion.

We need a more global algorithm. This is discouraging.

Maybe we can process the entire stack and reduce anywhere, not just top. But then we need an efficient way to find reductions. The rewriting system is local; we can use a stack but allow reductions that are not at the top? In a stack, reductions in the middle can be handled by a "reduce anywhere" algorithm if we maintain a list and a queue of potential reduction positions. This is like a string rewriting system; we can scan and whenever a reduction pattern appears, apply it, then check neighbors. This is similar to the leftmost reduction strategy. For V=1,2,1,3,2,3, after reading all, the reduction pattern 1,3,2,3 (free delete B) is at positions3-6. If we apply it, the type1 1,2,1,2 appears at positions1-4. A general algorithm that scans for any reduction pattern would find it. But which reduction to choose? We need maximize score. Maybe leftmost reduction strategy works? Let's test.

Leftmost reduction strategy:
Scan the current run sequence for the leftmost reducible pattern (type1 preferred over free? or any). Apply it, then restart/continue.

So maybe the optimal algorithm is simply: repeatedly apply the leftmost reducible pattern, preferring type1 over free deletion? Let's test on a case where leftmost free deletion might be bad.
