Leftmost reduction strategy seems plausible and is a standard way to compute a normal form for a terminating rewriting system. But is the system confluent with respect to score? We saw free deletion choice can affect score (V=1,2,1,3,2,3: leftmost free deletion good; deleting run6 bad). Leftmost gives good. Need prove leftmost is optimal. This is like "always reduce the leftmost redex" for a string rewriting system; for many systems, it yields a normal form that is optimal for a linear objective if rules are "non-overlapping" enough. Not sure.

Given the complexity, maybe the intended solution is indeed a simple stack with leftmost reduction, and we can implement it efficiently using a stack. A leftmost reduction can be implemented by scanning with a stack: when a reducible pattern appears at the top of the stack (which corresponds to the leftmost in the processed prefix), reduce. But we saw top-only missed a buried type1. However, if we process left to right and apply reductions as soon as they appear at the top, the leftmost reduction in the whole sequence will be applied when its rightmost element is processed. In V=1,2,1,3,2,3, the leftmost free deletion is at positions1-3, whose rightmost element is run3. When we processed run3, the stack was [1,2,1]. The free deletion pattern 1,2,1 with D=run4? At that time D (run4=3) was not yet read, so we didn't know if it's type1 or free. If we wait until run4, the pattern is at positions1-4, which is not at the top? Actually after reading run4, stack [1,2,1,3]; the pattern positions1-3 is at the bottom, not top. A top-only algorithm misses it. To implement leftmost reduction, we need remember that a reducible pattern at positions1-3 became available when run4 was read. This suggests using a queue of potential reduction positions, not just stack top.

We can implement a general reduction algorithm on a doubly linked list of runs with active flags, scanning for reducible patterns and applying them, then checking neighbors. Since total reductions O(N), we can maintain a list and a set of candidate positions. This is more complex but doable.

Let's design a list-based reduction:
- Represent current run sequence as a doubly linked list of nodes (value, active, prev, next).
- Initially all runs.
- We want to apply reductions (type1 or free) until no type1 remains? But free deletions only if they lead to type1? The leftmost strategy: At each step, find the leftmost reducible pattern. A reducible pattern is either:
   * Type1: four consecutive nodes A,B,C,D with B.active, C.active, A.val==C.val, B.val==D.val.
   * Free deletion: four consecutive nodes A,B,C,D with B.active, C.active, and (A.val==C.val, B.val!=D.val) OR (B.val==D.val, A.val!=C.val). Actually a free deletion can be applied if it is a redex. But applying a free deletion that doesn't lead to type1 is harmless (score0) but might destroy future type1? Leftmost strategy would apply it; in V=1,2,3,2,3, the leftmost redex is type1, so no free. In V=1,2,1,3,2,3, leftmost redex is free deletion at positions1-3? Wait positions1-4 1,2,1,3 is a free deletion redex (A==C, B!=D). Apply it. Good.
But in V=1,2,3,2,3, positions1-4 1,2,3,2 is a free deletion redex (B==D, A!=C). If we applied leftmost free deletion before checking type1, we'd lose score. So we must prioritize type1 over free deletion when finding leftmost redex. That is, find the leftmost type1 redex; if exists, apply it. Otherwise, find the leftmost free deletion redex and apply it. This matches leftmost strategy with priority.

Will this always maximize score? Let's test V=1,2,3,2,3: leftmost type1 at positions2-5, apply, score1. Good.
V=1,2,1,3,2,3: no type1, leftmost free at positions1-4, apply -> 1,2,3,2,3; then type1 -> score1. Good.
V=1,2,1,3,2,4,3,4: no type1, leftmost free at positions1-4 -> 1,2,3,2,4,3,4; no type1, leftmost free? positions2-5:2,3,2,4 free (A==C) -> 1,2,3,4,3,4; type1 -> score1. Good.

Now, is it possible that applying the leftmost free deletion is bad (reduces max score) when no type1 exists? We need find a counterexample. Suppose V=1,2,1,3,2,3,4,3,4. No type1? Actually type1 at runs6-9? 3,4,3,4 yes, so type1 priority would apply that first, not leftmost free. After applying rightmost type1, sequence 1,2,1,3,2,3, then leftmost free -> type1 -> score2. If instead leftmost free first: 1,2,3,2,3,4,3,4; then type1 on 2,3,2,3 -> 1,2,3,4,3,4; type1 on 3,4,3,4 -> 1,2,3,4 score2. Same.

Could leftmost free deletion remove a run needed for a future type1 that would give more score than the one it enables? Since free deletion itself gives no score, it only removes one run. If that run is part of a type1, then there would be a type1 redex currently (because the run with its matching neighbors would form a type1). Since no type1 exists, the run is not currently in a type1. It might be part of a future type1 after other deletions. Removing it could prevent that. But leftmost free deletion is the earliest possible; maybe it's safe by a "leftmost redex" theorem for optimal reduction in string rewriting with priorities. I'll trust.

Implementing leftmost reduction efficiently:
We can use a stack-based algorithm that simulates leftmost reduction. There is a known technique: process nodes left to right, maintain a stack. When a new node is pushed, check if the top of the stack forms a redex. Because the leftmost redex in the processed prefix will be at the top when its rightmost node is processed. But we saw in V=1,2,1,3,2,3, the leftmost free redex positions1-4 has rightmost node run4. When run4 is pushed, stack is [1,2,1,3]. The redex positions1-4 is the entire stack, so it IS at the top (the whole stack). A top check would see it! I earlier said top4 is 1,2,1,3, which is a free deletion redex. Our algorithm with "free deletion only if immediate type1" didn't apply it because immediate type1 false. But if we apply leftmost free deletion whenever it's a redex and no type1 at top, we would apply it. In V=1,2,3,2,3, after pushing run4, stack [1,2,3,2], top4 is a free deletion redex (B==D). If we apply it, we lose. But at that point, is there a type1 redex anywhere in the stack? No, because run5 not read. The leftmost reduction strategy with priority type1 would see no type1, so it would apply the free deletion at top, losing the future type1. But the optimal was to wait for run5. So a purely online leftmost reduction that reduces free deletions as soon as they appear is bad. We need delay free deletions when they might be part of a future type1. In V=1,2,3,2,3, the free deletion at top (1,2,3,2) overlaps with a future type1 that will appear after shifting run5. The free deletion removes B=2, which is needed for the type1. So we should not apply a free deletion if the next input could complete a type1. This requires lookahead of 1. In V=1,2,1,3,2,3, after pushing run4, free deletion at top (1,2,1,3) removes C=1, and the next input run5=2 does NOT complete a type1 with the current stack? Let's see if we delay, after run5 stack [1,2,1,3,2], no type1; after run6 stack [1,2,1,3,2,3], type1? top4 1,3,2,3 no; but there is a type1 after free deletion. If we don't free delete at run4, we can still free delete later? The free deletion at positions1-4 is no longer at top, but we could apply it in the middle. A leftmost reduction algorithm with lookahead might delay free deletion until it's clear it won't be part of a type1.

This is getting too deep.

Given time, maybe we should search for another solution. Let's consider using dynamic programming with min-cost max-flow? No.

Maybe the original problem has a much simpler answer: minimum operations = N - maximum number of pairs (i,j) such that A_i = A_j and i<j and ...? Let's try to compute answer for examples:
Sample1 N=5 answer3, savings2. Pairs of equal values: 1s at positions1,2,4 (3 pairs), 2s at3,5 (1 pair). Max non-overlapping pairs? Could select (1,2) and (3,5) savings? 2. Sample3 alternating N=11 answer8, savings3. Equal pairs: 1s 6 choose2=15, 2s 5 choose2=10. Max non-overlapping? Maybe maximum matching in a graph where edges connect equal values and intervals are disjoint? For alternating, can pair (1,3),(5,7),(9,11) for 1s and (2,4),(6,8) for 2s =5 edges, savings? Not 3. If require edges noncrossing? Pair (1,3),(5,7),(9,11) are noncrossing for 1s; 2s (2,4),(6,8) cross with 1s? Crossings count swaps. Savings = edges - crossings. If we take all 5 edges, crossings? Edges (1,3) and (2,4) cross, (5,7) and (6,8) cross, etc. 5 edges, 4 crossings? Savings1. Not 3. Maybe choose edges (1,3),(2,4)? crossings1, savings1. To save3 need 6 edges? Hmm.

What if answer = N - maximum size of a noncrossing matching in a graph where edges connect equal values and edges of different colors can cross but each crossing costs? This is the label model.

Let's try to solve label assignment via dynamic programming on values using the fact that N 2e5 but values up to N. Maybe we can use a segment tree to compute the minimum cost of a sequence of blocks. Consider the final D as a sequence of runs. The cost = runs + inversions. This is equivalent to: We want to partition the original sequence into blocks (labels) such that each block is monochromatic. This is exactly the label model. There is a known DP for "minimum cost to partition a sequence into colored blocks with inversion cost" that can be solved by maintaining for each value the minimum dp. Let's formulate.

Let dp[i] = minimum cost to delete the prefix up to i? But deletion blocks can contain non-contiguous elements, so not a simple prefix partition.

However, because labels per color nondecreasing, the elements assigned to each block are a subsequence. The order of blocks is a total order. This is equivalent to assigning each element a rank. We can think of building the final sequence D by merging the occurrence lists. The cost inversions can be computed incrementally: when we append a block of value x containing the next c occurrences of x to D, the additional inversions are the number of elements not yet appended that appear before these occurrences in A. This is exactly the queue process. We can choose the next block value and size. This is like we have N items, each value has a queue. We want to pop all items. Popping a block of c items of value x costs:
- deletion cost 1 (for the block)
- swap cost = number of remaining items of other values that are before the popped items in the original order.
We want minimize total. This is a shortest path problem on the state of pointers, but maybe there is an optimal greedy: always pop the value whose next occurrence is earliest? Let's test.

If we pop one item at a time, cost N. If we pop multiple, save c-1 deletions but pay swaps. The swap cost for popping c items of x equals the number of other remaining items before the last popped? Actually sum. If we pop the earliest remaining occurrence of x, cost0. Popping additional occurrences costs the number of other items between them. This is like we can "skip" over other items at cost. Maybe the optimal strategy is to always pop the value of the first remaining element, and pop as many of that value as possible until the swap cost exceeds the deletion savings? This is a greedy.

Let's test sample3 alternating. First element 1. Pop first 1 cost0. Next remaining starts 2. If pop value 1 again (next 1 at position3), cost = number of other items before it =1 (the 2). Saving deletion1, net0. Pop two 1s? cost1, deletion saving1, net0. Pop three 1s? cost3, saving2, net -1. So maybe pop just one? Then delete 2? Let's simulate greedy: pop first 1 (cost1 deletion), remaining starts 2. Pop first 2 (cost1), remaining starts 1. This is one-by-one cost11. Not optimal. If pop two 1s together cost swap1 + deletion1 =2 for two elements, same as one-by-one (2). No saving. Need pop two 1s and two 2s together to save. Greedy by value might not.

Maybe optimal block sizes are determined by a "matching" between adjacent equal values in the current sequence. This is too much.

Given the time, I think the run-reduction leftmost strategy is likely the intended solution. Let's implement a list-based reduction with priority type1 over free deletion, and hope. But we need be correct.

Let's test the leftmost strategy on V=1,2,3,2,3 with lookahead? The leftmost strategy with priority type1: after reading all, find leftmost type1 (positions2-5), apply. It doesn't apply free deletion at position1-4 because type1 exists. So online leftmost reduction that scans the whole current sequence, not reducing free deletions until no type1 exists, works. We can implement this by a simple algorithm:
- Maintain a list of runs.
- Loop:
   - Find the leftmost type1 redex in the list.
   - If found, apply it, score++, and restart (or continue from that position).
   - Else, find the leftmost free deletion redex.
   - If found, apply it (score unchanged), and restart.
   - Else break.
This is O(N^2) if naive, but we can use a stack/queue to apply reductions locally. However, N 2e5, O(N^2) too slow. But maybe the number of reductions is O(N) and we can find leftmost redex efficiently with a linked list and a priority queue of candidate positions.

We can maintain a doubly linked list of nodes. A redex is determined by a window of 4 consecutive nodes. We can initially check all windows and insert those that are type1 or free into a priority queue keyed by position (leftmost). When we apply a reduction, only windows near the changed area are affected; we can update candidates. Since each reduction removes at least one node, total O(N). We need prioritize type1 over free: if any type1 exists, we should apply the leftmost type1. If no type1, apply leftmost free. We can maintain two heaps: one for type1 redexes, one for free redexes. At each step, if type1 heap nonempty, pop leftmost valid type1 and apply. Else pop leftmost valid free and apply. This exactly implements leftmost priority.

Need define a window redex:
For four consecutive nodes A,B,C,D:
- Type1 if B.active and C.active and A.val == C.val and B.val == D.val.
- Free deletion if B.active and C.active and ((A.val == C.val and B.val != D.val) or (B.val == D.val and A.val != C.val)).
Note: If both A==C and B==D, it's type1, not free.
When applying type1:
- Remove C and D from the list.
- Mark A.active = False, B.active = False.
- Score++.
When applying free deletion:
- If A.val == C.val (and B.val != D.val): remove C, mark A.active = False.
- Else (B.val == D.val and A.val != C.val): remove B, mark D.active = False.
Wait in variant B, D absorbs B, so D.active=False. B removed. A remains active.
After removal, nodes around may have new neighbors; we need invalidate old candidate windows and add new ones.

This is a lot of implementation but feasible.

However, we must be sure the leftmost priority strategy is correct. Let's test with a potential counterexample where a type1 exists but applying a free deletion first would yield more score. Since type1 gives +1 and free gives0, delaying type1 to do free seems unlikely to increase total because free doesn't add score and might remove runs. But free could enable an additional type1 later, while doing type1 now might prevent it. Example: V=1,2,3,2,3 has type1 at positions2-5. If we do free deletion at positions1-4 first, we lose type1 (score0). So type1 priority is good. Could there be a case where doing a type1 now prevents two type1s later, while doing a free deletion first enables them? If type1 now removes runs that are needed for later type1s. But if those runs are needed, the later type1s would overlap with this type1. Overlapping type1s cannot both be done because type1 removes the right two runs and inactivates the left two. A later type1 could use the left two as A/B? Actually after type1, A,B remain inactive; they can be A/B in a future type1 (as in alternating). So type1 doesn't prevent future type1s that use A,B as outer; it only removes C,D. If a future type1 needed C or D as middle, it would overlap and likely be incompatible. So doing type1 early is safe. I'll trust.

Let's implement the list-based reduction. But before coding, let's test the algorithm on sample1:
A=1 1 2 1 2. RLE: runs [1(len2),2(len1),1(len1),2(len1)]? Actually A=1,1,2,1,2 -> runs: 1 len2, 2 len1, 1 len1, 2 len1. Values [1,2,1,2], active flags [False,True,True,True].
Windows:
1,2,1,2: B=2 active, C=1 active, A==C (1==1), B==D (2==2) -> type1. Apply: remove C,D (runs3,4), mark A.active=False, B.active=False. Score1. Remaining runs [1,2] inactive. No more. Answer = R0 - score = 4-1=3. Good.

Sample2: runs [4,2,1,3] all active. No type1/free? Check windows:
4,2,1,3: A==C?4==1 no; B==D?2==3 no. No. Score0, answer4. Good.

Sample3: alternating 11 runs all active. Algorithm with leftmost type1:
List 1,2,1,2,1,2,1,2,1,2,1.

Now implement list-based reduction with heaps. But maybe we can simplify using a stack that simulates leftmost reduction? The list-based approach is safer.

Data structures:
- Node: value, active, prev, next, index? We'll use arrays for nodes: val[i], active[i], prev[i], next[i].
- We need a way to identify windows. A window is represented by the first node index (A). Four consecutive nodes A,B,C,D where B=next[A], C=next[B], D=next[C].
- We'll maintain two heaps:
   - type_heap: min-heap of A indices for valid type1 windows.
   - free_heap: min-heap of A indices for valid free windows.
But when a node is removed or active changes, windows become invalid. We'll use a version/lazy validation: when popping a heap, check if the window is still valid (nodes exist, active flags, values match). If not, discard.

When we apply a reduction, we remove nodes and update active flags. Then we need to add new candidate windows that include the affected area. The affected area is around the removed nodes and the nodes whose active changed. We can simply, after each reduction, check all windows that start within a small range around the changes and add them to heaps. Since each reduction removes at least one node, total number of added windows O(N) if we only add windows that are new. But if we add duplicates, heap size O(N^2)? We can add a window multiple times; lazy validation will discard invalid ones. To avoid O(N^2), we can add windows only when they might be valid, and each window can be added at most a constant number of times? A window is determined by its A node. When nodes around change, a window with the same A may become valid again after being invalid. But A node remains; active flags of its successors can change. We might add it multiple times. However, each reduction changes active flags of at most two nodes and removes two nodes. A window's validity depends on active flags of B,C and values. It can become valid after a change. We can add all windows within a constant radius of changes; each reduction affects a constant number of nodes, so O(1) windows per reduction. A window might be added again later if affected again; a node can be affected multiple times? Active flags only change from True to False (never back). Nodes are removed once. So a window can become valid at most once? It could be valid, then invalid due to removal, then valid again with new successors? If its A remains and successors change, it's a different window (same A but different B,C,D). We should add it when successors change. A node can be involved in O(1) reductions? In alternating, a node can be A in multiple type1s? The same A node (e.g., run1) was A in first type1 and then remained; it was not A again? Actually run1 was A in first type1, then inactive; later not A. Run2 was A in second type1? It was A in window2-5. So a node can be A multiple times? In alternating, run2 was A in second type1 after being B in first? It was B in first, then A in second. So a node can be involved in multiple reductions. But total reductions O(N), and each reduction adds O(1) windows, so total heap insertions O(N). Good.

We need a helper `add_window(start)` that checks if start has four successors and if type1/free, pushes to heap. To avoid duplicates, we can just push; lazy validation handles.

After applying a reduction, we need know which nodes remain to add windows around them. We can add windows starting at:
- For type1: A, B, prev[A] (window starting before A), maybe prev[prev[A]]? Since removal of C,D and active changes of A,B affect windows that include A or B or their neighbors. The windows that can change are those whose start is within [prev[prev[A]], A, B, next[B]]? Let's be generous: add windows starting at prev[prev[A]], prev[A], A, B, next[B], next[next[B]] if exist. Since each window covers 4 nodes, changes at A/B affect windows starting at up to 3 positions before A/B. We can add a range of starts from prev[prev[A]] to B (inclusive) maybe. Simpler: after each reduction, collect a set of node indices that might be affected: the nodes remaining in the local area. We can add windows starting at each node from prev[prev[A]] up to B (or next[B]) by traversing prev pointers. Since local size constant, fine.

But we also need to remove invalid windows from heaps? Lazy validation: when popping, check if the window is still valid. If not, discard. This is fine.

Let's define `is_type1(start)`:
```
b = nxt[start]; if b == -1: return False
c = nxt[b]; if c == -1: return False
d = nxt[c]; if d == -1: return False
return active[b] and active[c] and val[start] == val[c] and val[b] == val[d]
```
`is_free(start)`:
```
if not (active[b] and active[c]): return False
if val[start] == val[c] and val[b] != val[d]: return True
if val[b] == val[d] and val[start] != val[c]: return True
return False
```
Note: If both conditions true, it's type1, not free. The second condition with val[start] != val[c] excludes type1. The first excludes type1 by val[b] != val[d]. Good.

Apply type1(start):
```
A=start, B=nxt[A], C=nxt[B], D=nxt[C]
# remove C and D
# link B to next[D]
nxt[B] = nxt[D]
if nxt[D] != -1: prev[nxt[D]] = B
active[A] = False
active[B] = False
score += 1
# nodes C,D are removed; we can leave their pointers, but they won't be reached.
```
Need also if D is last, nxt[B]=-1.

Apply free(start):
```
A=start, B=nxt[A], C=nxt[B], D=nxt[C]
if val[A] == val[C] and val[B] != val[D]:
    # remove C
    nxt[B] = nxt[D]  # since C removed, B connects to D
    if nxt[D] != -1: prev[nxt[D]] = B
    active[A] = False
else: # val[B] == val[D] and val[A] != val[C]
    # remove B
    nxt[A] = nxt[C]  # A connects to C
    if nxt[C] != -1: prev[nxt[C]] = A
    active[D] = False
```
Wait in variant B, original order A,B,C,D. Remove B -> A,C,D. So link A to C. D absorbs B, so D.active=False. Good.
In variant C, remove C -> A,B,D. Link B to D. A absorbs C, so A.active=False. Good.

After removal, we need add affected windows. We can write `add_around(node)` that adds windows starting at node and a few predecessors/successors. Since heaps are lazy, we can just add all possible starts in a small range:
```
def add_windows_from(start):
    for _ in range(4):
        if start == -1: break
        if nxt[start] != -1 and nxt[nxt[start]] != -1 and nxt[nxt[nxt[start]]] != -1:
            if is_type1(start): heappush(type_heap, start)
            if is_free(start): heappush(free_heap, start)
        start = prev[start]  # move left to include windows starting before
```
But after a reduction, affected windows start at nodes up to 3 positions before the changed area. We can just call `add_windows_from(A)` and `add_windows_from(B)` and maybe `add_windows_from(prev[A])`. To be safe, call for `A`, `prev[A]`, `prev[prev[A]]`, `B`, `next[B]`. Since local.

Initialization: Build linked list. For each node i from 0 to R-1, prev/next. Then for each start 0..R-4, add window.

Main loop:
```
while type_heap or free_heap:
    # clean type_heap top invalid
    while type_heap:
        start = type_heap[0]
        if is_valid_start(start) and is_type1(start): break
        heappop(type_heap)
    if type_heap:
        start = heappop(type_heap)
        apply_type1(start)
        add_affected(start)
        continue
    # no type1; clean free_heap
    while free_heap:
        start = free_heap[0]
        if is_valid_start(start) and is_free(start): break
        heappop(free_heap)
    if free_heap:
        start = heappop(free_heap)
        apply_free(start)
        add_affected(start)
        continue
    break
```
Need `is_valid_start(start)`: start not removed and has at least 3 successors. We can check nxt chain != -1. Removed nodes have nxt/prev maybe stale but won't be reached from head? We should ensure removed nodes are not in the linked list; their prev/nxt may still point but we never start from them if we only add existing nodes. Heap may contain start that was removed; is_valid_start should check that start is still in the list. We can maintain `alive[i]` boolean. Initially True; when removed, alive=False. Then is_valid_start requires alive[start] and alive[b] etc. Since if start removed, its nxt might still be set but we shouldn't use. So check alive for all four nodes.

When removing C and D in type1, set alive[C]=alive[D]=False. When free removing C or B, set alive accordingly.

`add_affected(start)` after reduction: We need add windows around the nodes that remain. We can call `add_windows_from(A)` and `add_windows_from(B)` and maybe `add_windows_from(prev[A])`. But if A or B removed? In type1 A,B remain; in free variant C A,B remain; variant B A,C,D remain (B removed). We'll call with the remaining nodes in the local area. Simpler: after any reduction, collect a list of nodes: A, B, prev[A], next[B] (or next of last remaining). Then call add_windows_from for each. Since add_windows_from moves left up to 3, duplicates okay.

But `add_windows_from` as written moves left; if we call for A and B, it covers starts before A. We need also starts after A? A window starting at B or next[B] might be affected. So call for B and next[B] too. We'll call for a set: prev[prev[A]], prev[A], A, B, next[B], next[next[B]] (if alive). Then add_windows_from each (which moves left). This might add many duplicates but O(1) per reduction.

Given time, I'll implement a simpler version: after each reduction, call `add_windows_from` for all nodes in a small list `[prev[prev[A]], prev[A], A, B, next[B], next[next[B]]]`, filtering alive.

Let's implement `add_windows_from(start)`:
```
def add_windows_from(start):
    cur = start
    for _ in range(4):
        if cur == -1 or not alive[cur]:
            break
        if nxt[cur] != -1 and nxt[nxt[cur]] != -1 and nxt[nxt[nxt[cur]]] != -1:
            b = nxt[cur]; c = nxt[b]; d = nxt[c]
            if alive[b] and alive[c] and alive[d]:
                if is_type1_node(cur,b,c,d): heappush(type_heap, cur)
                if is_free_node(cur,b,c,d): heappush(free_heap, cur)
        cur = prev[cur]
```
But if we call for A and B, we might add windows starting at A and predecessors. Windows starting after A (e.g., B) are added when we call for B. Good.

When popping from heap, we need validate:
```
def valid_type(start):
    if not alive[start]: return False
    b=nxt[start]; if b==-1 or not alive[b]: return False
    c=nxt[b]; if c==-1 or not alive[c]: return False
    d=nxt[c]; if d==-1 or not alive[d]: return False
    return active[b] and active[c] and val[start]==val[c] and val[b]==val[d]
```
Similarly free.

Heaps: Python heapq. Since we push duplicates, validation discards.

Complexity: Each reduction pushes O(1) windows. Initial O(R). Heap size O(R + reductions) = O(N). Good.

Now, we need be confident in the leftmost strategy. Let's try to find a counterexample by reasoning. Suppose there are two type1 redexes, leftmost and rightmost. Applying leftmost might prevent rightmost, but maybe applying rightmost first allows both? In alternating, applying leftmost allowed a chain; applying rightmost first (top4) gave only1. So leftmost is better. What about a case where leftmost type1 prevents a free deletion that would enable two type1s, while rightmost type1 allows them? Since type1 priority means we apply leftmost type1 before any free. Could that be suboptimal? Let's attempt to construct:
Sequence: type1 at left, and a free deletion in the middle that would enable two type1s on the right. If we do left type1, it might remove runs that are not needed for right; likely okay. If it removes runs needed for the free deletion, then the free deletion overlaps with the type1. Overlapping reductions often conflict; doing type1 gives +1, free gives0 but enables +2, net +2. Could be worse. Need find actual values.

Let's try to create overlapping type1 and free. Type1 on runs1-4: a,b,a,b. Free deletion on runs3-6: a,b,c,d? Since runs3,4 are a,b (active) and will be removed by type1, free deletion can't use them. So overlap means free deletion would be destroyed. Could free deletion on runs2-5: b,a,b,c? Type1 on runs1-4 removes runs3,4 (a,b), leaving runs1,2,5... Free deletion on runs2-5 originally would use runs2,3,4,5; after type1, runs3,4 gone, so impossible. If free deletion would enable two type1s, maybe doing type1 first still allows one of them? Hard.

Implementation details:
- Read T.
- For each case, read N, list A.
- Build RLE: runs as list of [value, length].
- If R <= 3, score=0? But there could be type1 with R=4. Our algorithm handles.
- Initialize arrays of size R:
  val = [v for v,l in runs]
  active = [l == 1 for v,l in runs]
  prev = [i-1 for i in range(R)]
  nxt = [i+1 for i in range(R)]; nxt[-1] = -1
  alive = [True]*R
- Heaps type_heap, free_heap = [], []
- Define functions using nonlocal? In Python, nested functions with lists are fine.

Potential issue: When we remove nodes, we don't update prev/nxt of removed nodes; but alive False prevents use. When linking, use alive? We only link alive nodes.

Let's code `add_windows_from(start)`:
```
def add_windows_from(start):
    cur = start
    for _ in range(4):
        if cur == -1 or not alive[cur]:
            return
        b = nxt[cur]
        if b != -1 and alive[b]:
            c = nxt[b]
            if c != -1 and alive[c]:
                d = nxt[c]
                if d != -1 and alive[d]:
                    if active[b] and active[c]:
                        if val[cur] == val[c] and val[b] == val[d]:
                            heapq.heappush(type_heap, cur)
                        elif val[cur] == val[c] and val[b] != val[d]:
                            heapq.heappush(free_heap, cur)
                        elif val[b] == val[d] and val[cur] != val[c]:
                            heapq.heappush(free_heap, cur)
        cur = prev[cur]
```
Note: If both val[cur]==val[c] and val[b]==val[d], type1; the `elif` excludes free. If val[cur]==val[c] and val[b]!=val[d], free variant C. If val[b]==val[d] and val[cur]!=val[c], free variant B.

Initial add: for i in range(R): add_windows_from(i) would add duplicates (each window from its start and predecessors). Better add for each start i from 0 to R-4 directly:
```
for i in range(R):
    add_windows_from(i)
```
This adds windows starting at i and also predecessors, causing duplicates but okay. Actually add_windows_from(i) adds windows starting at i, i-1, i-2, i-3. If we call for all i, each window added multiple times. O(R*4) okay. But to be clean, call for i in range(R) and accept duplicates; validation handles. Initial heap size O(4R). Fine.

After reduction, call add_windows_from on affected nodes. We need ensure we don't add windows starting at removed nodes; add_windows_from checks alive.

Apply type1:
```
def apply_type1(start):
    nonlocal score
    A = start; B = nxt[A]; C = nxt[B]; D = nxt[C]
    # remove C,D
    nxt[B] = nxt[D]
    if nxt[D] != -1:
        prev[nxt[D]] = B
    alive[C] = False; alive[D] = False
    active[A] = False; active[B] = False
    score += 1
    # add affected windows
    for x in (prev[A], A, B, nxt[B], nxt[nxt[B]] if nxt[B]!=-1 else -1):
        if x != -1 and alive[x]:
            add_windows_from(x)
```
But after linking, nxt[B] is the node after D. We should add windows starting at B and its next. Also A and prev[A]. Good.

Apply free:
```
def apply_free(start):
    A=start; B=nxt[A]; C=nxt[B]; D=nxt[C]
    if val[A] == val[C] and val[B] != val[D]:
        # remove C
        nxt[B] = nxt[D]
        if nxt[D] != -1:
            prev[nxt[D]] = B
        alive[C] = False
        active[A] = False
        affected = [prev[A], A, B, nxt[B]]
    else: # val[B] == val[D] and val[A] != val[C]
        # remove B
        nxt[A] = nxt[C]
        if nxt[C] != -1:
            prev[nxt[C]] = A
        alive[B] = False
        active[D] = False
        affected = [prev[A], A, C, D, nxt[D]]  # C remains, D remains
    for x in affected:
        if x != -1 and alive[x]:
            add_windows_from(x)
```
In variant B, after removing B, order A,C,D. We should add windows starting at A, C, D, and prev[A]. Also next[D]. Good.

Validation functions:
```
def valid_type(start):
    if not alive[start]: return False
    b=nxt[start]
    if b==-1 or not alive[b]: return False
    c=nxt[b]
    if c==-1 or not alive[c]: return False
    d=nxt[c]
    if d==-1 or not alive[d]: return False
    return active[b] and active[c] and val[start]==val[c] and val[b]==val[d]
```
Similarly free.

Main loop:
```
while True:
    while type_heap:
        start = type_heap[0]
        if valid_type(start):
            break
        heapq.heappop(type_heap)
    if type_heap:
        start = heapq.heappop(type_heap)
        apply_type1(start)
        continue
    while free_heap:
        start = free_heap[0]
        if valid_free(start):
            break
        heapq.heappop(free_heap)
    if free_heap:
        start = heapq.heappop(free_heap)
        apply_free(start)
        continue
    break
```
Note: After applying a reduction, some heap entries may become invalid; we clean lazily.

One subtlety: In `add_windows_from`, we push a window to type_heap if it's type1, and to free_heap if free. But a window could be both? We used if/elif, so type1 only in type_heap. Good.

Another subtlety: When active[A] set False in type1, A might be the start of a window where A is B or C in another window; those windows become invalid and will be discarded. New windows with A as A/D are added.

Now, answer = len(runs) - score. Since score counts type1 operations, each saves1. We should output that.

Let's consider if free deletions at the end could reduce final runs without affecting score, but answer formula R0 - score already accounts; final runs = R0 - 2score - free. Cost = swaps (score+free) + final runs = score+free + R0 -2score -free = R0 - score. So free deletions don't affect answer. We don't need to apply them at the end. Our algorithm only applies free deletions when they lead to type1 (via heap priority? Actually free_heap may contain free deletions that don't lead to type1; if no type1, we pop leftmost free and apply it, even if it doesn't lead to type1. This could reduce runs but not score; it might enable future type1s. If it doesn't, it's harmless but unnecessary. It might also destroy future type1s? The leftmost priority should avoid destroying type1s because type1s are applied first. If no type1 exists, applying a free deletion cannot destroy an existing type1. It might prevent a future type1, but leftmost strategy assumes not. In V=1,2,3,2,3, a free deletion existed and no type1 at that moment? Actually at the moment after reading all, type1 existed, so type_heap nonempty. If we processed online, free_heap had start0, type_heap had start1; type1 priority applied. Good. If we had applied free before type1, bad. So priority is crucial.

What if free_heap has a leftmost free that doesn't lead to type1, and a later free would lead to type1; leftmost might be bad. Example V=1,2,1,3,2,3 had two frees: start0 and start2. Leftmost start0 led to type1. Need find where leftmost free bad. Suppose V=1,2,1,4,3,4,3? No type1. Free deletions: start0 (1,2,1,4) leftmost; if apply ->1,2,4,3,4,3. Then type1? 3,4,3? no, 4,3,4,3 at end? Sequence 1,2,4,3,4,3 has runs4-6? 4,3,4,3? Actually 1,2,4,3,4,3 -> last four 4,3,4,3 type1! So leftmost good. If instead free at end (3,4,3) would also? Hard.

I'll trust.

Let's now write the final code in the required format. Need be careful with recursion? None.

Potential performance: T up to 1e5, sum N 2e5. Building RLE O(N). Heaps O(N log N). Fine.

Let's produce final answer with code block.
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import heapq

def solve_case(A):
    # Run-length encoding
    runs = []
    for x in A:
        if runs and runs[-1][0] == x:
            runs[-1][1] += 1
        else:
            runs.append([x, 1])

    R = len(runs)
    if R < 4:
        return R

    val = [v for v, _ in runs]
    active = [l == 1 for _, l in runs]  # True iff run length is 1
    prev = [i - 1 for i in range(R)]
    nxt = [i + 1 for i in range(R)]
    nxt[-1] = -1
    alive = [True] * R

    type_heap = []
    free_heap = []

    def is_valid_type(start):
        if not alive[start]:
            return False
        b = nxt[start]
        if b == -1 or not alive[b]:
            return False
        c = nxt[b]
        if c == -1 or not alive[c]:
            return False
        d = nxt[c]
        if d == -1 or not alive[d]:
            return False
        return active[b] and active[c] and val[start] == val[c] and val[b] == val[d]

    def is_valid_free(start):
        if not alive[start]:
            return False
        b = nxt[start]
        if b == -1 or not alive[b]:
            return False
        c = nxt[b]
        if c == -1 or not alive[c]:
            return False
        d = nxt[c]
        if d == -1 or not alive[d]:
            return False
        if not (active[b] and active[c]):
            return False
        if val[start] == val[c] and val[b] != val[d]:
            return True
        if val[b] == val[d] and val[start] != val[c]:
            return True
        return False

    def add_windows_from(start):
        cur = start
        for _ in range(4):
            if cur == -1 or not alive[cur]:
                return
            b = nxt[cur]
            if b != -1 and alive[b]:
                c = nxt[b]
                if c != -1 and alive[c]:
                    d = nxt[c]
                    if d != -1 and alive[d]:
                        if active[b] and active[c]:
                            if val[cur] == val[c] and val[b] == val[d]:
                                heapq.heappush(type_heap, cur)
                            elif val[cur] == val[c] and val[b] != val[d]:
                                heapq.heappush(free_heap, cur)
                            elif val[b] == val[d] and val[cur] != val[c]:
                                heapq.heappush(free_heap, cur)
            cur = prev[cur]

    # initial windows
    for i in range(R):
        add_windows_from(i)

    score = 0

    def apply_type1(start):
        nonlocal score
        A = start
        B = nxt[A]
        C = nxt[B]
        D = nxt[C]

        # remove C and D
        nxt[B] = nxt[D]
        if nxt[D] != -1:
            prev[nxt[D]] = B
        alive[C] = False
        alive[D] = False

        # A and B become non-singleton
        active[A] = False
        active[B] = False
        score += 1

        affected = [prev[A], A, B, nxt[B]]
        if nxt[B] != -1:
            affected.append(nxt[nxt[B]])
        for x in affected:
            if x != -1 and alive[x]:
                add_windows_from(x)

    def apply_free(start):
        A = start
        B = nxt[A]
        C = nxt[B]
        D = nxt[C]

        if val[A] == val[C] and val[B] != val[D]:
            # remove C, A absorbs C
            nxt[B] = nxt[D]
            if nxt[D] != -1:
                prev[nxt[D]] = B
            alive[C] = False
            active[A] = False
            affected = [prev[A], A, B, nxt[B]]
        else:
            # remove B, D absorbs B
            nxt[A] = nxt[C]
            if nxt[C] != -1:
                prev[nxt[C]] = A
            alive[B] = False
            active[D] = False
            affected = [prev[A], A, C, D, nxt[D]]

        for x in affected:
            if x != -1 and alive[x]:
                add_windows_from(x)

    while True:
        # Prefer type-1 reductions.
        while type_heap:
            st = type_heap[0]
            if is_valid_type(st):
                break
            heapq.heappop(type_heap)

        if type_heap:
            st = heapq.heappop(type_heap)
            apply_type1(st)
            continue

        # Otherwise use a free reduction.
        while free_heap:
            st = free_heap[0]
            if is_valid_free(st):
                break
            heapq.heappop(free_heap)

        if free_heap:
            st = heapq.heappop(free_heap)
            apply_free(st)
            continue

        break

    return R - score


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    t = data[0]
    idx = 1
    out = []
    for _ in range(t):
        n = data[idx]
        idx += 1
        A = data[idx:idx + n]
        idx += n
        out.append(str(solve_case(A)))
    print("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
